Book context: Queried - the cinema chapter's history section in full, from the Lumiere exhibition to the establishment of the Elphinstone Picture House and the first Hollywood agency; cross-read against the theatre chapter's record of early motion-picture screening in Bengal and of the first permanent cinema theatre in Madras
CA search: "Not sought - this is a dated chronology lesson built on the core source"
CA found: None sought; the session's single current record is held for Lesson 13
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the arrival timeline, with the exact kind of "first" at each step

```text
 YEAR   EVENT                                    WHAT KIND OF FIRST IS THIS?
 ----   -----                                    ---------------------------
 1896 | Lumiere Brothers show SIX silent      | first EXHIBITION of motion
      | films in Bombay                       | pictures in India
      |                                       | (not an Indian film at all)
        |
 1897 | Coconut Fair and Our Indian Empire    | first film SHOT in India
      |                                       | (not made by an Indian)
        |
 1899 | Harishchandra Bhatavdekar, "Save      | first motion VENTURE BY AN
      | Dada": two short films, exhibited     | INDIAN (shorts, not a feature)
        |
 1900 | Major Warwick establishes the first   | first CINEMA HOUSE in Madras
      | cinema house in Madras                | (exhibition infrastructure)
        |
 1900s| F.B. Thanawalla: Taboot Procession;   | early Indian film-making, no
      | Splendid New Views of Bombay          | "first" claimed
        |
 1903 | Hiralal Sen: Indian Life and Scenes   | a named early Indian picture
        |
 1907 | Jamshedji Madan establishes the       | first INDIAN-OWNED picture
      | Elphinstone Picture House, Calcutta   | house named in the source
        |
 1912 | Pundalik - N.G. Chitre and            | first INDO-BRITISH
      | R.G. Torney                           | COLLABORATION
        |
 1913 | Raja Harishchandra - Dadasaheb        | first INDIGENOUS INDIAN
      | Phalke                                | SILENT FEATURE
        |
 1916 | Universal Studios opens the first     | first foreign studio AGENCY
      | Hollywood-based agency in India       | in India

 THE SHAPE OF THE PERIOD, IN ONE LINE:
 +---------------------------------------------------------------+
 | EXHIBITION (1896) -> SHOOTING (1897) -> INDIAN AUTHORSHIP      |
 | (1899) -> PERMANENT VENUES (1900, 1907) -> COLLABORATION       |
 | (1912) -> INDIGENOUS FEATURE (1913) -> FOREIGN CAPITAL (1916)  |
 |                                                               |
 | Seventeen years from a foreign novelty to an Indian feature.   |
 | The infrastructure of SHOWING films preceded the industry of   |
 | MAKING them - which is why the early landmarks are venues      |
 | and importers as often as they are directors.                  |
 +---------------------------------------------------------------+
```

The right-hand column is the entire discipline of this lesson. There are at least six different "firsts" inside seventeen years, and each one is a first *of a different thing*. A statement-elimination item does not need to invent anything to be difficult here; it only needs to move one of these labels one row.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Everybody remembers 1913 and Phalke. Almost nobody remembers that by 1913 India had already had seventeen years of moving pictures, two permanent cinema houses, several Indian film-makers and an Indo-British co-production. The consequence is a compressed and wrong mental picture in which Indian cinema *begins* with *Raja Harishchandra*, and every earlier fact, when it appears in an option, looks like an invention.

The correction is not more names. It is a **shape**: the medium arrived as an imported entertainment, was being shot in India within a year, was being made by Indians within three, had permanent venues within four, and produced an indigenous feature within seventeen. Hold the shape and the individual facts have somewhere to sit.

### 2. 1896-1899: the imported novelty and its immediate indigenisation

The **Lumiere Brothers** introduced motion pictures to India, showing **six silent films in Bombay in 1896**. That is the arrival of the *medium*, and it is the row most often misread: it is not an Indian film, not an Indian exhibition venture, and not a production event.

The first **Indian-shot** film, *Coconut Fair and Our Indian Empire*, appeared in **1897** - one year later. Note precisely what this establishes: that India was being *filmed* almost immediately. It does not establish Indian authorship.

Indian authorship arrives in **1899**, when **Harishchandra Bhatavdekar**, popularly known as **Save Dada**, made the first motion venture by an Indian - **two short films**, which he exhibited to an audience. Two qualifications travel with this row and both are examinable. They were **shorts**, not a feature, which is why 1913 remains a distinct landmark rather than a repetition. And Bhatavdekar both made and **exhibited** them, which is characteristic of the period: making and showing were not yet separate trades.

In the **1900s** there were very few Indian film-makers. The source names **F.B. Thanawalla**, who made *Taboot Procession* and *Splendid New Views of Bombay*, and **Hiralal Sen**, well known for *Indian Life and Scenes*, made in **1903**. Both titles are worth noticing for what they are: processions, views, scenes of life. Early Indian film-making, like early film-making everywhere, was **actuality** before it was narrative.

### 3. 1900-1907: why the venues are landmarks

Because the pictures were temporary exhibits, as the market for them increased they created an urgent need for a cinema house. That need was met by **Major Warwick**, who established the **first cinema house in Madras, now Chennai, in 1900**. Later, a wealthy Indian businessman, **Jamshedji Madan**, established the **Elphinstone Picture House in Calcutta, now Kolkata, in 1907**.

This pair of rows carries the most under-used analytical point in the whole chronology. The early history of Indian cinema is an **exhibition** history before it is a production history. Before there was an Indian film industry there was an Indian film *audience*, built by importers and venue-owners, and it was the existence of that paying audience that made indigenous production commercially conceivable. A candidate who narrates only directors has removed the demand side of the story and, with it, the explanation of why 1913 was possible.

**A discrepancy inside the source, recorded and not resolved.** The cinema chapter states that Major Warwick established the first cinema house in Madras in 1900. The theatre chapter of the same source separately states that the **Gaiety Theatre** was the **first permanent cinema theatre set up in the then Madras**, by **Mr Raghupathy Venkaiah**, and attaches no year to that claim. The two statements are not obviously consistent, and this session does not resolve them. Both are recorded, the qualifier *permanent* is noted as the possible reconciling term, and neither is used as a decisive "first" in any model answer.

**A second cross-chapter record, kept as given.** The theatre chapter states that the **first motion pictures in Bengal**, made by **Hira Lala Sen**, were screened at **The Star Theatre**, the **Minerva Theatre** and **The Classic Theatre**. The cinema chapter spells the same person **Hiralal Sen**. The spelling variance is recorded rather than silently normalised. The substantive point is more interesting than the spelling: early film exhibition in Calcutta happened **inside existing theatres**, which is the physical form of the continuity between stage and screen that Lesson 7 develops.

### 4. 1912-1913: collaboration, then the indigenous feature

The **first Indo-British collaboration** came in **1912** with ***Pundalik***, by **N.G. Chitre** and **R.G. Torney**. It is the immediate predecessor of the landmark that follows it, and its presence is what makes the 1913 claim precise rather than sweeping: 1913 is the first *indigenous* feature, and 1912 is why the word *indigenous* is doing work.

**Dadasaheb Phalke** produced ***Raja Harishchandra*** in **1913**, and the source describes it as the **first indigenous Indian silent film**. He is known as the **father of Indian cinema** and is credited with *Mohini Bhasmasur* and *Satyavan Savitri*, and with the **first box-office hit**, ***Lanka Dahan***, in **1917**.

**A second source-internal variance, recorded.** The same source's awards chapter describes Phalke as the legendary film-maker who made **India's first full-length feature film, *Raja Harishchandra*, in 1913**. The cinema chapter's formulation is *first indigenous Indian silent film*. These are close but not identical claims - one emphasises indigeneity and the silent form, the other emphasises feature length. This session uses the cinema chapter's wording in the chronology, records the awards chapter's wording where the award is discussed in Lesson 13, and does not merge them into a third formulation of its own.

**What the subject matter tells us.** *Raja Harishchandra*, *Mohini Bhasmasur*, *Satyavan Savitri* and *Lanka Dahan* are all drawn from the mythological and epic repertoire. That is the single most important fact about the content of early Indian cinema and it is usually skipped. The technology was imported and the **subject matter was not**: the first Indian features localised the medium by pouring into it the narrative material that Indian audiences already possessed from performance, recitation and print. Indigenisation, in other words, happened at the level of **story** before it happened at the level of technique.

### 5. 1916: foreign capital arrives, which is also part of the story

Looking at the profits in the budding Indian market, **Universal Studios established the first Hollywood-based agency in India in 1916**. It belongs in the chronology because it corrects a sentimental reading of the period. The Indian film market was not a sheltered national development; within twenty years of the medium's arrival it was attractive enough for a foreign studio to open an agency in it. Indian production grew inside a market that was **simultaneously** being supplied from abroad, and that competitive condition is part of why the indigenous industry organised itself into companies so quickly, as Lesson 4 records.

### 6. Comparative treatment: the six firsts, with the exact trap each one anchors

| The claim | The exact fact | The statement that would be false |
|---|---|---|
| First motion pictures in India | Lumiere Brothers, **six silent films**, Bombay, **1896** | "The first Indian film was made in 1896" |
| First film shot in India | *Coconut Fair and Our Indian Empire*, **1897** | "The first film made by an Indian appeared in 1897" |
| First venture by an Indian | **Harishchandra Bhatavdekar (Save Dada)**, two shorts, **1899** | "Phalke made the first film by an Indian" |
| First cinema house in Madras | **Major Warwick**, **1900** - with the Gaiety Theatre claim recorded beside it | "India's first cinema house was in Bombay" |
| First Indo-British collaboration | ***Pundalik***, **1912**, **N.G. Chitre and R.G. Torney** | "*Raja Harishchandra* was India's first film of any kind" |
| First indigenous Indian silent feature | ***Raja Harishchandra***, **1913**, **Dadasaheb Phalke** | "Indian cinema began in 1913" |
| First box-office hit | ***Lanka Dahan***, **1917** | "*Raja Harishchandra* was the first commercial success" |

The reason to hold the right-hand column rather than only the middle one is that the middle column is what you revise and the right-hand column is what you are shown. Every false statement in that column is built by taking a true fact and moving it one row.

### 7. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** This chronology is a list of colonial-era novelties and Indian imitations. Treating 1896 as the beginning of Indian cinema makes Indian film history derivative by construction, and ignores the far longer Indian traditions of visual narrative, shadow play and performed storytelling from which Indian cinema really descends.

**Reply.** The objection identifies a real distortion and the concession should be made explicitly: the *narrative and performative* resources of Indian cinema are indigenous and old, which is precisely why the first features were mythologicals and why film song, as Lesson 9 shows, was structurally central from the moment sound arrived. But the conclusion does not follow, for two reasons. First, cinema is defined by a **technology** - photographic recording, projection, reproducibility - and that technology genuinely did arrive in 1896 rather than developing indigenously; dating its arrival honestly is not the same as conceding cultural dependence. Second, the chronology read carefully makes the **opposite** case to the one the objection fears: an imported technology was being shot in India within a year, made by Indians within three, housed in Indian-owned venues within eleven and carrying Indian mythological narrative within seventeen. That is a record of **rapid appropriation**, not of imitation. The defensible position is the indigenisation thesis: the formative achievement of Indian cinema was the speed with which an imported technology was made to carry Indian content, and the evidence for it is the very chronology the objection mistrusts.

### Examination use

Any question asking a candidate to *trace the development of Indian cinema* is answered from this lesson and the next two, and the marks lie in **dated, differentiated firsts**, not in an undated list of milestones. The **indigenisation thesis** in section 7 is the strongest available opening for such a question because it converts a chronology into an argument. In an objective item, this is the densest field of paired traps in the topic: exhibition against production, shooting against authorship, shorts against feature, collaboration against indigenous, and first film against first hit.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating 1896 as the date of the first Indian film.
- Attributing the first Indian venture to Phalke rather than to Bhatavdekar.
- Forgetting that Bhatavdekar's 1899 work was **two short films**, not a feature.
- Collapsing *Pundalik* (1912) into *Raja Harishchandra* (1913).
- Writing that *Raja Harishchandra* was the first box-office hit; that is *Lanka Dahan*, 1917.
- Omitting the exhibition landmarks and narrating only film-makers.
- Resolving the two Madras first-cinema-house claims by picking one silently.

### Revision notes

1. **1896** - Lumiere Brothers, **six silent films**, Bombay: the first **exhibition** of motion pictures in India.
2. **1897** - *Coconut Fair and Our Indian Empire*: the first film **shot** in India.
3. **1899** - **Harishchandra Bhatavdekar**, known as **Save Dada**: first motion venture **by an Indian**, **two short films**, exhibited by him.
4. **1900s** - **F.B. Thanawalla**: *Taboot Procession*, *Splendid New Views of Bombay*. **1903** - **Hiralal Sen**: *Indian Life and Scenes*.
5. **1900** - **Major Warwick**, first cinema house in **Madras**. The theatre chapter separately names the **Gaiety Theatre**, set up by **Raghupathy Venkaiah**, as the first permanent cinema theatre there, without a year; both are recorded.
6. **1907** - **Jamshedji Madan**, **Elphinstone Picture House**, Calcutta.
7. **1912** - ***Pundalik***, **N.G. Chitre and R.G. Torney**: first **Indo-British collaboration**.
8. **1913** - ***Raja Harishchandra***, **Dadasaheb Phalke**: first **indigenous Indian silent** feature; he is the **father of Indian cinema**, also credited with *Mohini Bhasmasur* and *Satyavan Savitri*.
9. **1917** - ***Lanka Dahan***: first **box-office hit**.
10. **1916** - **Universal Studios**, first Hollywood-based **agency** in India.
11. The early landmarks are **venues and importers** as often as film-makers: exhibition infrastructure preceded production.
12. The first Indian features were **mythologicals**: indigenisation happened in **story** before technique.
13. The shape to remember: exhibition 1896 -> shooting 1897 -> Indian authorship 1899 -> venues 1900 and 1907 -> collaboration 1912 -> indigenous feature 1913.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The formative achievement of early Indian cinema was the speed of its indigenisation." Discuss with reference to the period 1896-1917. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is defensible; the chronology proves it.

**The medium was imported.** The Lumiere Brothers showed six silent films in Bombay in **1896**; a foreign studio opened an agency by **1916**.

**Appropriation was immediate.** India was being filmed within a year - *Coconut Fair and Our Indian Empire*, **1897**. An Indian was making films within three: **Harishchandra Bhatavdekar**, two shorts, **1899**. Indian-owned exhibition followed - **Elphinstone Picture House**, Calcutta, **1907**, after Madras's first cinema house, **1900**. An Indo-British collaboration, ***Pundalik***, came in **1912**, and the first indigenous feature, ***Raja Harishchandra***, in **1913**.

**The decisive evidence is content, not speed alone.** Phalke's subjects - *Raja Harishchandra*, *Mohini Bhasmasur*, *Satyavan Savitri*, and the first hit *Lanka Dahan* (**1917**) - are mythological. Indigenisation occurred at the level of narrative material before technique.

**The qualification:** exhibition preceded production and infrastructure stayed partly foreign-supplied, so this was appropriation within a competitive market, not autonomous invention.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the imported origin stated with the 1896 exhibition 1 + at least four correctly dated and correctly *labelled* firsts between 1897 and 1913 4 + the mythological subject matter used as the evidence of content-level indigenisation 3 + the exhibition-before-production observation 1 + the competitive-market qualification 1.** Conflating any two of the firsts - exhibition with production, or shorts with feature - costs two marks each time, because the argument depends entirely on their being distinct events.

### Concept check

**Question:** An option states: "Dadasaheb Phalke made the first Indian film, *Raja Harishchandra*, which was also the first Indian box-office success." Identify every error and give the corrected chronology.

**Model answer:** There are **three errors** in a single sentence. **First, "the first Indian film."** The first motion pictures exhibited in India were the **six silent films** shown by the **Lumiere Brothers** in Bombay in **1896**; the first film **shot** in India was *Coconut Fair and Our Indian Empire*, **1897**; and the first motion venture **by an Indian** was by **Harishchandra Bhatavdekar**, who made **two short films** in **1899** and exhibited them. Phalke's film is the first **indigenous Indian silent feature**, which is a narrower and different claim. **Second, the implied exclusivity of 1913.** An **Indo-British collaboration**, ***Pundalik*** by **N.G. Chitre and R.G. Torney**, preceded it in **1912**, which is exactly why the word *indigenous* is load-bearing in the 1913 description. **Third, "the first Indian box-office success."** The source attributes the first box-office hit to ***Lanka Dahan***, **1917**, also by Phalke - a different film, four years later. The corrected chronology therefore runs: **1896** exhibition; **1897** first Indian-shot film; **1899** first venture by an Indian, two shorts; **1900** first cinema house in Madras; **1903** Hiralal Sen's *Indian Life and Scenes*; **1907** Elphinstone Picture House, Calcutta; **1912** *Pundalik*; **1913** *Raja Harishchandra*; **1917** *Lanka Dahan*. Phalke's standing as **father of Indian cinema** survives all three corrections intact - it rests on the indigenous feature and on the body of work that followed, not on being first at everything.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that the most famous landmark in a chronology is also the earliest one. Fame and priority are different properties, and in this period they come apart repeatedly - the best-remembered name is not the first film-maker, the best-remembered film is not the first film, and the first film is not the first success.

## Lesson 4 - The silent era, the companies, and the State's first look at the screen

Progress: 4/16 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: 1910-1920s - live musical accompaniment, the production companies of 1918, entertainment tax, the film-makers the companies enabled, the first Indian woman producer-director, and the Indian Cinematograph Committee of 1927

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the cinema chapter's complete silent-films section, including live accompaniment, the 1918 companies, the entertainment tax, the named film-makers and the 1926 landmark; the chapter's record of the 1927 colonial committee; and the theatre chapter's record of Parsi producers moving into film-making
CA search: "Not sought - this is a period lesson built on the core source's silent-era section"
CA found: None sought; the session's single current record is held for Lesson 13
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the silent-era machine - four parts that assembled themselves within a decade

```text
 ================== 1. THE SCREEN WAS NEVER SILENT ==================
   "silent films" 1910-1920        accompanied LIVE by
        |                          sarangi | tabla | harmonium | violin
        +--> the consequence: from the very beginning, the Indian
             film experience was an AUDIO-VISUAL one, and the music
             was Indian even when the technology was not
                                 |
 ================== 2. PRODUCTION ORGANISES ITSELF ==================
   1918 : Kohinoor Film Company
   1918 : Dadasaheb Phalke's Hindustan Cinema Films Company
        |
        +--> companies, not one-off ventures: standing capital,
             repeat production, and a place for new film-makers
        +--> the film-makers the companies enabled:
             Baburao Painter | Suchet Singh | V. Shantaram
                                 |
 ================== 3. THE STATE NOTICES THE MONEY =================
   1922 : Entertainment Tax imposed in Calcutta
   1923 : Entertainment Tax imposed in Bombay
        |
        +--> the first governmental relationship with cinema is
             FISCAL. Taxation precedes regulation by five years.
                                 |
 ================== 4. THE STATE NOTICES THE CONTENT ===============
   1927 : INDIAN CINEMATOGRAPH COMMITTEE, founded by the British Raj
        |  - to look into the ADEQUACY of censorship
        |  - to scrutinise the "immoral effect" of cinematograph films
        +--> state anxiety about cinema's mass reach is OLDER than
             independence; the 1952 statute inherits a concern, not
             an empty field

 AND, RUNNING THROUGH ALL FOUR:
 1926 : FATMA BEGUM - first Indian woman to produce AND direct her
        own film, Bulbul-e-Paristan. Note both credits: producing and
        directing, in the silent era, before the talkie.
```

Read the four bands downward. They are not four unrelated facts; they are a sequence with a logic. Music was present from the start; production organised into companies; money appeared and was taxed; and once cinema was demonstrably reaching large audiences and earning, the colonial State asked what it was showing them. That order - **audience, then industry, then revenue, then regulation** - is the shape of the period and the reason the regulatory story in Lessons 10 and 11 begins where it does.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

"Silent era" sounds like a gap to be got through on the way to 1931. Treated that way it yields nothing but two or three orphan dates. Treated properly it is the decade in which Indian cinema acquired the three things that made everything afterwards possible: an audience habit, a company structure, and a State that had noticed it.

There is also a specific correction to make at the start, because it changes how the whole period is imagined.

### 2. The silent films were not silent

The decade of **1910-1920** was dominated by silent films. Although they were called silent films, they were **accompanied by live music** on instruments like the **sarangi, tabla, harmonium and violin**.

Take the consequence seriously rather than filing the fact. Three things follow.

**First, the Indian film experience was audio-visual from the beginning.** There was never a period in which an Indian audience sat in front of a purely visual narrative. When recorded sound arrived in 1931 it did not introduce music to Indian cinema; it *internalised* music that had always been there, moving it from the hall into the print.

**Second, the accompaniment was Indian.** The instruments named are instruments of Indian musical practice. The technology was imported; the sound in the room was not. This is the clearest early instance of the indigenisation argument from Lesson 3 operating at the level of *experience* rather than of subject matter.

**Third, this is the structural explanation of film song.** The question usually asked as a puzzle - why is Indian popular cinema so saturated with song? - has part of its answer here. The audience's habit of receiving film *with* music predates the technical possibility of recording it. Lesson 9 takes this further; this is where the fact enters.

### 3. 1918: the film-making process gets an impetus

The process of film-making got an impetus in **1918** by the opening of two film companies: the **Kohinoor Film Company** and **Dadasaheb Phalke's Hindustan Cinema Films Company**.

What the word *company* changes is worth spelling out, because it is the step from ventures to an industry. A company means standing capital rather than per-film financing; continuity of equipment and personnel; and, decisively, a **place for a new film-maker to work** without having to raise his own money for each picture. The source says exactly this: the film companies provided an opportunity to many film-makers such as **Baburao Painter**, **Suchet Singh** and **V. Shantaram**.

Hold the causal direction. The companies did not appear because there were film-makers; the film-makers became possible because there were companies. That is also why the studio system of the 1930s, in Lesson 5, is an *intensification* of 1918 rather than a novelty.

### 4. 1922 and 1923: the first governmental relationship with cinema is fiscal

Once films began making a decent amount of money, the government imposed the **Entertainment Tax** in **Calcutta in 1922** and the next year, **1923**, in **Bombay**.

This is a small fact that carries a large point, and it is almost always omitted. The State's first substantive engagement with cinema in India was not censorship and not promotion; it was **taxation**. It came *because* films were profitable, it came in the two principal exhibition cities, and it came five years before the colonial government constituted a committee to examine what films were showing. An answer that presents the State-cinema relationship as beginning with censorship has the sequence wrong; the honest sequence is revenue first, content second.

The federal echo is worth noticing too. Entertainment taxation has remained, historically, a matter imposed at the level of the provinces and later the States, while certification became a Union subject. The structural split between **who takes the money from exhibition** and **who permits exhibition** appears here in embryo and is formalised in Lesson 10.

### 5. 1926: Fatma Begum, and the exact credit

**Fatma Begum** was the **first Indian woman who produced and directed her own film**, in **1926**, titled ***Bulbul-e-Paristan***.

Three precisions, because this row is frequently mis-stated.

- The claim is **produce and direct**, and both credits are part of it. It is not a claim about the first Indian woman to appear on screen, which is a different claim that this session does not make because no held source supports it.
- The film is ***Bulbul-e-Paristan***, and the year is **1926** - in the **silent era**, before sound.
- She is not an appendix to the period. A woman producing and directing her own film in 1926 is a fact about the openness of a young industry that had not yet hardened its hierarchies, and it sits in productive tension with the representational history in Lesson 8, where the *images* of women on screen are recorded as moving in a much less linear way than this single fact would suggest.

### 6. 1927: the Indian Cinematograph Committee, and what it tells us

The **Indian Cinematograph Committee** was founded by the **British Raj** in **1927**. It aimed to look into the **adequacy of censorship** and to scrutinise the **"immoral effect" of cinematograph films**.

Read the two stated aims carefully, because they are not the same aim.

The first - the *adequacy* of censorship - presupposes that censorship already existed and asks whether it was sufficient. The second - the *immoral effect* of films - is a statement about the medium's power over audiences, and it is the premise from which almost all subsequent regulation of cinema anywhere has proceeded: that moving images shown to large numbers of people do something to those people.

Two conclusions follow, and both are answer material.

**The colonial State's anxiety about cinema was about mass reach.** Nothing comparable was constituted for painting, sculpture or literature. What made cinema a governmental problem was the number of people it reached simultaneously and the fact that many of them were not literate - the medium's reach did not depend on the audience's education. That is the property identified in Lesson 1 as *mass simultaneity*, now producing its predictable political consequence.

**The 1952 Act inherits rather than invents.** When independent India enacted a certification statute, it was not entering an empty field; a colonial apparatus of film censorship and a colonial inquiry into its adequacy already existed. This matters for the evaluation in Lesson 11: the Indian scheme's continuity with a colonial concern about the effect of films on audiences is part of the honest assessment of it, and cannot be settled either by defending the Board or by denouncing it.

### 7. The bridge from stage to screen, recorded with its date

The theatre chapter of the core source records that **Parsi theatres** were famous in western India during the **1850s-1920s**, with plays in Gujarati and Marathi, colourful backdrops and music, and themes of romance, humour and melodrama - and that **from the 1930s, with the advent of cinema, many Parsi producers went into film-making**.

The date is the point. The migration of personnel from the Parsi stage into film is placed by the source in the **1930s**, which is the decade of sound, not of the silent era. That is exactly what one would expect: a theatrical tradition built on dialogue, song and melodrama had far more to contribute to a medium that could record speech and music than to one that could not. This session therefore records the stage-to-screen migration here, as the *continuity* that explains the conventions of the next lesson's cinema, while keeping its stated date in the 1930s rather than backdating it into the silent period.

The theatre chapter also records the **Indian People's Theatre Association**, formed in **1943** as a cultural wing of the Communist Party, disbanded in **1947**, whose named associated personalities include **Balraj Sahni**, **Prithviraj Kapoor**, **Bijon Bhattacharya**, **Ritwik Ghatak** and **Utpal Dutt**, and that it now exists in **Chhattisgarh, Punjab and West Bengal**. Two of those names - Ghatak and Kapoor - return in Lesson 7, which is where the significance of a politically committed theatre movement feeding into cinema is argued rather than merely noted.

### 8. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** Calling 1922 the State's first substantive engagement with cinema is an over-reading of a tax. Taxation is what governments do to any profitable activity; it says nothing specific about cinema and certainly nothing about culture.

**Reply.** The generality of taxation is exactly the concession to make: nothing about an entertainment tax is culturally specific, and an answer that treats it as a cultural policy has over-claimed. But the fact retains two uses that survive the objection. First, as **evidence of scale**: a tax is imposed where there is revenue to take, so the 1922 and 1923 impositions are independent confirmation that by the early 1920s Indian film exhibition was commercially substantial - a firmer indicator than any claim about popularity. Second, as **sequence**: the point is not that the tax was culturally significant but that it came *before* the content inquiry of 1927, which corrects the common narrative in which the State's relationship with cinema begins with censorship. The defensible formulation is therefore narrow and worth keeping: the tax is evidence of the industry's commercial weight and a marker of chronological order, not a statement of cultural policy.

### Examination use

This lesson supplies the middle of any *trace the development* answer, and it supplies the two analytical observations that such answers usually lack: that Indian cinema was **audio-visual from the outset** through live accompaniment, and that the State's engagement ran **revenue before content**. The **1927 Committee** is the origin point for any question on film censorship or certification and should be named whenever the Cinematograph Act is discussed, because it establishes that the 1952 statute inherited a colonial concern. **Fatma Begum, 1926**, is a high-value and frequently omitted identification, and the exact credit - produced *and* directed - is the part that is tested.

### Traps introduced here

- Describing the silent era as genuinely silent, and so missing the live accompaniment on sarangi, tabla, harmonium and violin.
- Dating the two film companies to any year other than **1918**, or attributing Hindustan Cinema Films Company to anyone but Phalke.
- Reversing the entertainment tax cities: **Calcutta 1922**, **Bombay 1923**.
- Stating Fatma Begum's achievement as acting or as a post-sound event, rather than as producing and directing in **1926**.
- Treating the **1927** body as a censor board; it was a **committee** constituted to examine the adequacy of censorship.
- Backdating the migration of Parsi theatre producers into film; the source places it **from the 1930s**.
- Presenting the State's relationship with cinema as beginning with censorship.

### Revision notes

1. **1910-1920** was dominated by silent films, **accompanied by live music** on **sarangi, tabla, harmonium and violin**.
2. Consequence: the Indian film experience was **audio-visual from the start**, the accompaniment was **Indian**, and 1931 internalised music rather than introducing it.
3. **1918** - two film companies opened: the **Kohinoor Film Company** and Phalke's **Hindustan Cinema Films Company**.
4. Companies enabled film-makers, not the reverse; the source names **Baburao Painter**, **Suchet Singh** and **V. Shantaram**.
5. **Entertainment Tax**: **Calcutta 1922**, **Bombay 1923**. The State's first substantive engagement with cinema was **fiscal**.
6. Taxation (1922-23) preceded the content inquiry (1927) by five years.
7. **1926** - **Fatma Begum**, first Indian woman to **produce and direct** her own film, ***Bulbul-e-Paristan***, in the silent era.
8. **1927** - **Indian Cinematograph Committee**, founded by the **British Raj**, to examine the **adequacy of censorship** and scrutinise the **"immoral effect"** of cinematograph films.
9. Cinema became a governmental problem because of **mass reach** and independence from literacy - no comparable body existed for other art forms.
10. The **1952 Act inherits** a colonial apparatus and a colonial anxiety; it does not create regulation from nothing.
11. **Parsi theatre**, famous in western India **1850s-1920s**; **from the 1930s** many Parsi producers moved into film-making.
12. **IPTA**, formed **1943** as a cultural wing of the Communist Party, disbanded **1947**; associated names include **Balraj Sahni, Prithviraj Kapoor, Bijon Bhattacharya, Ritwik Ghatak, Utpal Dutt**; it now exists in **Chhattisgarh, Punjab and West Bengal**.
13. The period's shape: **audience -> industry -> revenue -> regulation**.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Examine the relationship between the colonial state and Indian cinema up to 1927, and assess what that relationship bequeathed to independent India. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The relationship developed in two movements, in a revealing order.

**First, revenue.** Once films earned, **Entertainment Tax** came in **Calcutta, 1922** and **Bombay, 1923**. This is the State's earliest substantive engagement, and evidence of commercial scale: a tax follows money. It carries no cultural judgement.

**Second, content.** The **Indian Cinematograph Committee** was founded by the **British Raj** in **1927** with two stated aims - to examine the **adequacy of censorship**, and to scrutinise the **"immoral effect"** of cinematograph films. The first aim presupposes an existing censorship; the second states the premise on which film regulation has rested ever since, that moving images shown to large audiences act upon them.

**Why cinema and not other arts.** No comparable inquiry attended painting, sculpture or literature. The difference is **mass simultaneity** and independence from literacy: cinema reached very large numbers at once. Anxiety tracked reach, not merit.

**The bequest is threefold.** Independent India inherited, first, an **existing apparatus** of film censorship rather than an empty field; second, a **rationale** framed in terms of effect on audiences, which reappears in the 1952 scheme's grounds of offence to a geographical area, age group, religious denomination or political group; and third, a **structural split** between provincial revenue from exhibition and central concern with content, which survives as the federal division between Union certification and State enforcement.

**The qualification:** continuity of apparatus is not identity of purpose; the post-1950 scheme is a certification regime answerable to constitutional constraints the colonial arrangement was not.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the revenue-before-content sequence with both tax years and cities 3 + the 1927 Committee named with **both** its stated aims 4 + the mass-reach explanation of why cinema and not other arts 3 + three distinct bequests identified, including the federal split 4 + the continuity-is-not-identity qualification 1.** Describing the 1927 Committee as a censor board, or as the body that certified films, forfeits three marks: it was constituted to examine the adequacy of censorship, which is a different institutional act.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Because films were silent until 1931, music entered Indian cinema only with *Alam-Ara*." Explain why this is wrong and what follows from the correction for understanding Indian popular cinema.

**Model answer:** The sentence is wrong at its premise and therefore wrong in its conclusion. The films of **1910-1920** were called silent, but they were **accompanied by live music** in the hall, on **sarangi, tabla, harmonium and violin**. Music was therefore never absent from the Indian film experience; what 1931 changed was where the music was *located* - it moved from live performance in the auditorium into the recorded print. **Three consequences follow.** First, the arrival of sound did not have to *create* an audience appetite for film music; it inherited one that was already a decade and a half old, which is part of why song became structurally central to Indian popular cinema rather than an occasional decoration. Second, the accompanying instruments were **Indian**, so the film experience was culturally localised at the level of sound long before it was technically Indian at the level of recording - a clear instance of indigenisation working through experience. Third, the correction reframes 1931 itself: *Alam-Ara* is the first **talking** film, and its examinable associated fact is that **W.M. Khan**'s song **"De de khuda ke naam par"** from that film was the **first recorded song** in Indian cinema, captured **live on set**. Note the precision required here - *first recorded* song, not first song; and a *singing* credit, not an acting or music-direction credit. Note also what must **not** be carried across: the core source's further description of Khan as India's first **playback singer** is reported rather than relied on, because playback proper - a song pre-recorded and lip-synced on set - is associated with **1935**, not with the first talkie. The candidate's error, in short, converts a change in the **technology of reproduction** into a change in the **presence of music**, and in doing so loses the best available explanation of why Indian cinema sings.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading a technical label as a description of experience. "Silent film" names what the print carried, not what the audience heard, and the same trap recurs later in this topic wherever an administrative label - certification, merger, listing - is mistaken for a description of what actually happens.
