```text
   - every DUBBED version,    |  - television programmes and serials,
     which needs a FRESH      |    governed by the Content/
     certificate              |    Advertisement Code under the Cable
                              |    Television Network (Regulation)
                              |    Act, 1995 - whose offences are
                              |    NON-COGNIZABLE
```

Read the bottom panel twice. Two of the topic's sharpest objective distinctions live there: the **federal split** between who certifies and who enforces, and the **offence asymmetry** - cognisable and non-bailable under the film statute, non-cognizable under the cable statute.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Candidates arrive at this lesson with a single word - *censorship* - and a general attitude toward it, and then discover that the examinable material is almost entirely structural: which Act, which Board, when founded, when renamed, under which ministry, how many members, appointed by whom, on what grounds, with what three possible outcomes, enforced by whom, applying to what, and not applying to what.

The attitude is not useless - section 7 shows exactly where it becomes an argument - but it has to come *after* the structure. An answer that argues about artistic freedom without being able to state the composition limits of the Board has nothing for the examiner to mark.

### 2. The statute, and the precise thing it was for

The Government of India instituted the **Indian Cinematograph Act, 1952**, **to certify films**. The **major function** of the Act was to **flesh out the constitution and the functioning of the Central Board of Film Certification**, also called the Censor Board of India.

Two precisions matter.

**The stated purpose is certification.** Not censorship, not regulation of the industry, not promotion. The Act's own object, as the source states it, is to certify films - which is the textual basis for the analytical distinction in section 7.

**The Act's major function is institutional.** It is the statute that constitutes the Board and defines how it works. That is why almost every examinable fact in this lesson is a fact about the Board: the Act's principal content *is* the Board.

### 3. The Board: founding, renaming, ministry and composition

- **Founded in 1950** as the **Central Board of Film Censors**.
- **Renamed** under the Act of **1952**; the current name is the **Central Board of Film Certification**.
- It is **directly under the directive of the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting**.
- Its **head office is in Mumbai**, with regional offices that deal specifically with regional films. The core source names offices at **Delhi, Kolkata, Chennai, Bengaluru, Guwahati, Cuttack, Thiruvananthapuram and Hyderabad**; the canonical position used in this session records **nine** regional offices - **Mumbai, Kolkata, Chennai, Bengaluru, Thiruvananthapuram, Hyderabad, New Delhi, Cuttack and Guwahati** - together with a **regional facilitation office at Chandigarh**. The count variance is recorded in the ledger and is not smoothed over.
- **Composition:** the Act provides for the appointment of a **Chairman** and a team of **not less than 12 and not more than 25** members, appointed by the **Central Government**, to assist the Chairman.
- **Appointment and tenure:** the Chairman and governing members are appointed by the government **through the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting**, and can be appointed for a term of **three years or more**, as per the government directive. The members are usually **well-known personalities from the film industry or other intellectuals**.

**The three high-frequency facts in that list** are the **1950 founding under the older name**, the **12-to-25 range**, and the **direct subordination to the ministry**. Each is a clean objective target, and the first is also an argumentative resource: a body founded as a board of *censors* and renamed as a board of *certification* carries its own history in its name-change, which is the shortest possible way into the analysis in section 7.

### 4. What the Board actually does: the grounds and the three outcomes

**The Board has to examine the film** and decide if the film should **not** be exhibited on the grounds of offence to a certain **geographical area**, **age group**, **religious denomination** or **political group**.

Four grounds, and they are worth reading as a set rather than as a list. Three of them - geographical area, religious denomination, political group - are about **communities that might be offended**; one - age group - is about **audiences that might be harmed**. That is not one principle but two, and the distinction matters because the age-based grounds are the ones that survive most comfortably in a modern justification of the regime, while the community-offence grounds are the ones that generate the hardest questions about expression. An answer that separates them is doing something almost no candidate does.

**Three outcomes are available to the Board**, and an answer should name all three because candidates usually name only the extremes:

1. **Certify** the film.
2. **Direct the applicant to make modifications and excisions** in the film before a certificate is given.
3. **Refuse to sanction** the film for public exhibition, if such changes are not made.

Outcome 2 is the analytically important one and is the one most often omitted. It is neither permission nor prohibition; it is **conditional permission**, and it is the mechanism through which a certification system acquires a censoring effect without ever formally banning anything.

### 5. The federal split, and the reach of the regime

**The federal split.** Although **certification of films is a subject under the Union**, the **enforcement of the censorship in their respective domain lies with the State governments**.

This single sentence is the most examinable structural fact in the lesson, and its consequence is worth drawing out. A film may hold a valid central certificate and still encounter State-level action affecting its exhibition, because the two functions sit at different levels of government. Lesson 4 showed the embryo of the same split, with exhibition taxed provincially while content anxiety was central. An answer that states the split and names its consequence - that a central certificate is a necessary condition for exhibition but is administered against a background of State enforcement - is answering a question most candidates do not realise is there.

**What the regime covers.**

- **All films** require a certificate before they can be screened in cinema theatres.
- **Imported foreign films** must obtain CBFC certification.
- **Every dubbed version** - a film dubbed from one language to another - must obtain a **fresh certificate**, to ensure that the language change is not offensive in any manner.

**What the regime does not cover.**

- Films made **especially for Doordarshan**, which, as the official broadcaster of the Government of India, has **its own set of rules** for examining such films. This is stated as the **only** exception to the certificate requirement.
- **Television programmes and serials**, for which CBFC certification is **not required**. These are governed by the **Content/Advertisement Code** prescribed for programmes and advertisements on the cable television network under the **Cable Television Network (Regulation) Act, 1995**.

**The offence asymmetry, which is a favourite discrimination.** Exhibition of **uncertified films** is a **cognisable and non-bailable** offence under the Cinematograph Act, 1952. Offences under the **Cable Television Network Regulation Act** are **non-cognizable**. The two statutes treat their respective violations with markedly different severity, and an option that reverses them is the standard trap.

### 6. Comparative treatment: certification against three things it is not

| Certification is not... | Because... |
|---|---|
| **An award** | it is a legal precondition for exhibition, granted on statutory grounds; a jury's judgement of merit has no part in it |
| **A recommendation** | a certificate says the film may lawfully be shown to a defined audience, not that it is worth showing |
| **A licence to the film-maker** | it attaches to the **film** and to its **public exhibition**; it is not a qualification held by a person |

And the comparison that matters most:

| Axis | **Certification**, as the scheme is framed | **Censorship**, in the strict sense |
|---|---|---|
| Object | the audience for which a film is suitable | the content of the film itself |
| Typical act | classify, and allow exhibition to that audience | delete, alter or suppress |
| Outcome if the standard is not met | a more restrictive category | excision, or prohibition |
| Stated Indian position | the Act was instituted **to certify films** | - |
| Actual Indian powers | the Board may **direct modifications and excisions** and may **refuse to sanction** | these are censoring powers |

The table is the argument of the next section in compressed form: the scheme is **certification in its stated object** and carries **censoring powers in its operative provisions**, and the honest answer says both.

### 7. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** Calling the Indian scheme "certification" is a euphemism. A body that can order cuts and can refuse to let a film be shown at all is a censor, whatever the statute calls it, and the change of name from Board of Film Censors to Board of Film Certification in 1952 was a change of vocabulary rather than of function.

**Reply.** A large part of this must be conceded, and conceding it is what makes the rest credible. The Board's operative powers - to **direct modifications and excisions**, and to **refuse to sanction** a film for public exhibition if the changes are not made - are censoring powers in the ordinary meaning of the term, and no amount of terminology alters that. But three things block the conclusion that the distinction is empty. **First, the default differs.** A censorship regime's characteristic act is suppression; a certification regime's characteristic act is classification, and the overwhelming majority of films pass through by being placed in a category rather than by being cut or refused. **Second, one of the four statutory grounds is genuinely classificatory.** Offence to an **age group** is not an argument for suppressing a film but for restricting its audience, and a system that matches films to audiences is doing something different in kind from one that removes content from circulation. **Third, the distinction has practical consequences**: it determines what an applicant can do - comply, contest, or accept a narrower category - and it is the reason an appeal route exists at all, which Lesson 11 traces. **The defensible verdict**, which is also the most markable one, is therefore: *India's scheme is certification in form and partly censorship in effect - the Board classifies films by audience, but its power to require excisions and to refuse sanction means the distinction holds legally more firmly than it holds practically.* That sentence concedes the objection's force, keeps the distinction, and answers the question.

### Examination use

Every question on film censorship, certification, the CBFC or artistic freedom is answered from this lesson and the next. The mark-bearing spine is: **statute and its stated object -> the Board's founding, renaming and ministry -> composition and appointment -> the four grounds -> the three outcomes -> the federal split -> the scope and its exceptions -> the certification-against-censorship verdict.** In an objective item the recurring targets are the **1950 founding under the older name**, the **12-to-25** range, the **federal split**, the **fresh certificate for dubbed versions**, the **Doordarshan exception**, and the **cognisable/non-cognizable asymmetry** between the film and cable statutes. Note also the boundary: the constitutional doctrine of free speech and the case law on film censorship belong to the polity owner, and an answer should say so rather than attempt it.

### Traps introduced here

- Calling the body the Censor Board as if that were its current name, or dating its founding to 1952 rather than **1950**.
- Misstating the composition as a fixed number instead of **not less than 12 and not more than 25**.
- Naming only two outcomes and omitting **modifications and excisions**, which is the analytically decisive one.
- Reversing the federal split - certification is **Union**, enforcement of censorship is with the **States**.
- Forgetting that **imported** films and every **dubbed** version need certification, the latter a **fresh** one.
- Extending the Doordarshan exception to television generally; TV programmes and serials are covered by a **different statute**, not by an exception to this one.
- Reversing the offence asymmetry: **cognisable and non-bailable** under the film statute, **non-cognizable** under the cable statute.

### Revision notes

1. The **Cinematograph Act, 1952** was instituted **to certify films**; its major function was to flesh out the **constitution and functioning of the CBFC**.
2. The Board was **set up in 1950** as the **Central Board of Film Censors** and **renamed** under the **1952** Act.
3. It is **directly under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting**; head office **Mumbai**; **nine** regional offices plus a **regional facilitation office at Chandigarh**, with the core source naming eight regional offices beside Mumbai.
4. **Composition:** a **Chairman** plus **not less than 12 and not more than 25** members, appointed by the **Central Government**.
5. **Tenure:** appointments through the **Ministry of Information and Broadcasting**, for **three years or more** as per government directive; members are usually film-industry personalities or other intellectuals.
6. **Four grounds** on which the Board may decide a film should not be exhibited: offence to a **geographical area**, **age group**, **religious denomination** or **political group**. Three are community-offence grounds; one is an audience-protection ground.
7. **Three outcomes:** certify; **direct modifications and excisions**; **refuse to sanction** if the changes are not made.
8. **Federal split:** certification is a **Union** subject; **enforcement of censorship** in their respective domains lies with the **State governments**.
9. **Scope:** all films for public exhibition; **imported foreign films**; every **dubbed** version needs a **fresh certificate**.
10. **Only stated exception:** films made **especially for Doordarshan**, which has its own rules.
11. **Outside the regime:** **television programmes and serials**, governed by the **Content/Advertisement Code** under the **Cable Television Network (Regulation) Act, 1995**.
12. **Offence asymmetry:** exhibiting an **uncertified film** is **cognisable and non-bailable** under the 1952 Act; offences under the cable statute are **non-cognizable**.
13. **The verdict line:** certification **in form**, partly censorship **in effect** - the distinction holds legally more firmly than practically.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"India's film certification regime is certification in form and censorship in effect." Critically examine. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition captures a real tension, with a stated limit.

**The form is certification.** The **Cinematograph Act, 1952** was instituted **to certify films**, and its major function was to constitute the **Central Board of Film Certification** - set up in **1950** as the Board of Film **Censors**, renamed under that Act, **directly under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting**, with a **Chairman and not less than 12 and not more than 25 members**. Its characteristic act is to place a film in a category for a defined audience; a certificate is a legal precondition for exhibition, and exhibiting an uncertified film is **cognisable and non-bailable**.

**The effect is partly censorship.** The Board may **direct modifications and excisions**, and may **refuse to sanction** a film for public exhibition if those changes are not made. Those are censoring powers, and three of the four grounds - offence to a **geographical area, religious denomination or political group** - concern content, not audiences.

**The limit on the proposition.** The fourth ground, offence to an **age group**, is genuinely classificatory: restricting an audience is different in kind from removing content from circulation. Most films pass by categorisation, not excision.

**A structural complication** deepens the tension: **certification is a Union subject**, while **enforcement of censorship lies with the State governments**, so a validly certified film operates against a second, decentralised layer of control.

**Verdict:** certification in form and partly censorship in effect - a distinction that holds legally more firmly than it holds practically.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the statute's stated object and the Board's institutional facts, with founding, renaming, ministry and composition 4 + all three outcomes named, with modifications and excisions identified as the decisive one 3 + the four grounds split into community-offence and audience-protection 3 + the federal split stated with its consequence 2 + a qualified verdict that concedes the censoring effect while preserving the distinction 3.** Writing "the CBFC censors films" without the classification limb forfeits three marks; so does asserting any constitutional test of reasonable restriction, which belongs to another owner and is not established here.

### Concept check

**Question:** A film made in one language is certified, then dubbed into another language and released; a television serial with comparable content is broadcast without any certificate; and a State government subsequently acts against the dubbed film's exhibition. Explain the legal position at each stage.

**Model answer:** **Stage one - the original film.** It required a certificate from the **Central Board of Film Certification** before public exhibition, because **all films** need one; exhibiting it without a certificate would have been a **cognisable and non-bailable** offence under the **Cinematograph Act, 1952**. The Board examined it and had three options - certify, **direct modifications and excisions**, or **refuse to sanction** if the changes were not made. **Stage two - the dubbed version.** It is **not covered by the original certificate**. A film dubbed from one language into another must obtain a **fresh certificate**, to ensure that the language change is not offensive in any manner. The law treats the re-languaged film as a new object requiring its own examination, which is also the sharpest administrative evidence that Indian cinema is administered as many language markets. **Stage three - the television serial.** **CBFC certification is not required for television programmes and serials.** They fall instead under the **Content/Advertisement Code** prescribed for programmes and advertisements on the cable television network under the **Cable Television Network (Regulation) Act, 1995**, whose offences are **non-cognizable** - a markedly lighter treatment than the film statute's. The separate and narrower exception within the film regime is for films made **especially for Doordarshan**, which has its own rules. **Stage four - the State action.** This follows from the federal division: **certification is a subject under the Union**, but the **enforcement of censorship in their respective domain lies with the State governments**. A valid central certificate is therefore a necessary condition for lawful public exhibition, administered against a background of State-level enforcement, and the two operate at different levels of government. The constitutional questions this raises about expression belong to the polity owner and are not resolved here.

**Misconception to avoid:** Imagining that one certificate settles the legal position of a film everywhere and in every version. It does not travel across languages, it does not extend to television, and it does not displace State enforcement - three separate limits, each with its own instrument.

The currentness panel is the most valuable thing in this lesson. Four statements that appear in circulating study material are no longer accurate, and each of them is exactly the kind of statement an objective item is built on.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

This is the one part of the topic where **revising from an older source actively damages you**. Almost everything else here - the 1913 chronology, the silent era, the parallel movement - is stable. Film law is not. In the space of a few years the appellate tribunal was abolished, the certificate categories were re-cut, the validity of certificates changed and a ministerial power was removed.

The solution is not to distrust the older material, which is accurate about the period it describes. It is to hold the law as a **ladder with dates**, so that every statement carries the rung it belongs to and you can always say whether a given proposition is current or historical. A candidate who can say "that was the position until 2021" is in a far stronger place than one who has simply memorised the newest facts, because the examiner can test either end.

### 2. Rungs 1 to 3: from an inquiry to a statute

**1927 - the Indian Cinematograph Committee**, founded by the **British Raj**, to look into the **adequacy of censorship** and to scrutinise the **"immoral effect"** of cinematograph films. Lesson 4 treated this in detail. Its place on the ladder is as the demonstration that the field was not empty in 1950.

**1950 - the Central Board of Film Censors** is set up. Note that this precedes the Act by two years: the body existed before the statute that now governs it, which is why "founded in 1952" is wrong and "founded in 1950 under a different name" is right.

**1952 - the Cinematograph Act**, instituted **to certify films**, whose major function was to flesh out the **constitution and functioning** of the Board, and under which the Board was **renamed** the Central Board of Film Certification. At this rung the categories are **U - universal exhibition** and **A - restricted to adult audience only**.

Two categories. That is worth registering, because the whole subsequent history of categories is a story of **subdivision**, and understanding it as subdivision makes the current markers easy to hold.

### 3. Rung 4: 1983, and the logic of subdivision

In **1983** there was an **upgradation in the Cinematograph (Certification) Rules** which added **two more certification categories** to the existing ones:

- **UA** - unrestricted public exhibition **subject to parental guidance for children under the age of 12 years**;
- **S** - public exhibition **restricted to a specialised audience such as doctors, engineers** and the like.

Look at what the two additions do. They are not two more of the same thing; they solve two different problems that a two-category system cannot.

**UA solves the intermediate case.** Between "anyone may watch" and "adults only" there is a large class of films that most adults would let older children watch with guidance. Without an intermediate category, such films must be pushed into one of the extremes, which either over-restricts or under-warns.

**S solves the specialised case.** A film made for a professional audience - medical, technical - is not adult material in the ordinary sense; it is material whose appropriate audience is defined by **occupation** rather than by age. That is a different axis entirely, which is why it needed its own category rather than a finer age band.

Hold that difference, because the 2023 amendment changes the UA axis and leaves the S axis alone, and knowing why tells you immediately which categories survived.

**Note also the instrument.** The 1983 change was made through **rules**, not through an amendment to the Act. Rules are subordinate legislation, easier to revise than a statute - which is exactly the property that makes a certification scheme adjustable. An answer that notices the difference between a statutory amendment and a change in rules is showing an understanding of how the regime is actually maintained.

### 4. Rung 5: 1995, a separate regime rather than a new rung of the same one

The **Cable Television Network (Regulation) Act, 1995** prescribes a **Content/Advertisement Code** for programmes and advertisements appearing on the cable television network, and its offences are **non-cognizable**.

This is placed on the ladder deliberately, even though it is not a film-certification statute, because its relation to the film regime is a recurring examination point. It does not extend certification to television; it creates a **parallel and lighter regime for a different medium**, and the asymmetry of offences - cognisable and non-bailable under the film statute, non-cognizable under the cable statute - is the measure of how differently the two media were treated. Lesson 15 shows the same structural move happening again a quarter-century later for online curated content.

### 5. Rung 6: 2021, the tribunal is removed

Section **5D** of the Cinematograph Act had constituted the **Film Certification Appellate Tribunal**, set up specifically to hear appeals from parties seeking re-examination of the Board's decision. The core source describes this as **another major provision** of the 1952 Act.

That provision no longer operates. **Section 5D was omitted by the Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021, with retrospective effect from 4 April 2021.** The **current appeal route is section 5C**, which permits an appeal to the **High Court**.

Three things follow and all three are examinable.

**The appellate layer moved from a specialised tribunal to a constitutional court.** That is a change in the *kind* of forum, not merely in its name: a tribunal staffed for the purpose is replaced by a court of general jurisdiction.

**The printed literature is now wrong on this point.** Any source describing the appellate tribunal as the current route - including the core source used throughout this session - is describing the position before 2021. This session records that explicitly rather than silently correcting it, because knowing *what changed* is more useful than knowing only the current answer.

**Do not describe the tribunal as abolished by the film statute.** It was removed by a general tribunals-reform statute that rationalised a large number of tribunals; the change reached film certification as part of that wider exercise rather than as a film-policy decision. The mechanism is part of the fact.

### 6. Rung 7: the 2023 amendment, item by item

The **Cinematograph (Amendment) Act, 2023** is the current top of the ladder. Its content is best held as four separate moves.

**Move 1 - the categories are re-cut on the age axis.** The amended Act **retains U, A and S** and replaces the single **UA** category with three age-marked categories: **UA 7+**, **UA 13+** and **UA 16+**. The **age endorsement within the UA category informs the guidance of parents or guardians, and is not enforceable by any person other than parents or guardians**.

That last clause is the one to hold. The marker is **advisory to the guardian**, not a legal bar enforced against anybody. An option that turns UA 13+ into an age restriction enforceable at the box office has misdescribed it. Notice too that the change is confined to the UA axis, exactly as section 3 predicted: **U, A and S survive unchanged**, because the problems they solve were not the problem being addressed.

**Move 2 - a separate certificate for television and other prescribed media.** Films carrying an **'A' or 'S' certificate require a separate certificate** for exhibition on television or any other media prescribed by the central government, and the Board may direct appropriate deletions or modifications for that separate certificate. This is a genuine extension of the certification idea across media, and it sits interestingly beside the older rule that television programmes and serials themselves need no CBFC certificate: the *film* crossing into television is treated differently from television's own content.

**Move 3 - unauthorised recording and exhibition become offences.** The amendment prohibits **carrying out or abetting the unauthorised recording and the unauthorised exhibition of films**, and **attempting** an unauthorised recording is also an offence. Unauthorised recording means making or transmitting an infringing copy of a film at a licensed exhibition place without the owner's authorisation; unauthorised exhibition means the public exhibition of an infringing copy for profit, at an unlicensed location or in a manner infringing copyright law. Certain **exemptions under the Copyright Act, 1957** apply - limited use without the owner's authorisation for **private or personal use**, **reporting of current affairs**, or **review or critique** of the work. The punishment is **imprisonment between three months and three years** and a **fine between three lakh rupees and five per cent of the audited gross production cost**.

**Move 4 - validity and ministerial power.** Certificates, previously valid for **ten years**, are now **perpetually valid**. And the central government's power to **examine and make orders in relation to films certified or pending certification**, which the Board was required to give effect to, has been **removed**.

Move 4 is the one with the largest constitutional flavour and the smallest word count, so it is easy to miss. Removing a revisional power held by the executive over a statutory Board's certification decisions increases the Board's autonomy in the ordinary sense of the word - and it does so in the same statute that also extends the regime across media and creates new offences. An honest answer notes both directions at once rather than characterising the amendment as simply liberalising or simply tightening.

**A provenance note, stated plainly.** The **category markers** and the **section 5C appeal to the High Court**, together with the **omission of section 5D**, are taken from the canonical owner's record of the official consolidated statutory text. The remaining items in this section - the separate television certificate, the two piracy offences with their exemptions and punishment, the shift to perpetual validity and the removal of the revisional power - are taken from a bounded check of a legislative-research summary of the amending legislation, and are recorded as such in the ledger. **No section number is asserted for the piracy provisions**, and no notification, rule-making date or implementing rule is claimed anywhere in this session.

### 7. Comparative treatment: the categories, then and now

| Axis | Position in 1952 | Position after 1983 | Position after 2023 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Unrestricted | **U** | **U** | **U** |
| Intermediate, guidance-based | - | **UA** - parental guidance for children **under 12** | **UA 7+**, **UA 13+**, **UA 16+** - age endorsements informing **parents or guardians**, not enforceable by others |
| Adults only | **A** | **A** | **A** |
| Occupation-defined audience | - | **S** - specialised audience, e.g. doctors, engineers | **S** |

Read across the second row only: that is the entire history of category change in one line. Two categories became four in 1983; the intermediate one became three in 2023; the other three have never changed. Anyone who holds that sentence can reconstruct the whole table.

### 8. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** Reciting a ladder of statutes and rules is administrative bookkeeping. The interesting question about film law is whether the State should be classifying and cutting films at all, and a chronological account evades it.

**Reply.** The evasion charge would stick if the ladder were offered as the whole answer, and it is not. But the ladder is what makes the interesting question answerable rather than merely arguable, for three reasons. **First, it separates what is defended from what is asserted.** Someone who wishes to defend the regime is defending a scheme whose intermediate categories are advisory to guardians and whose specialised category is occupational; someone who wishes to attack it is attacking a power to require excisions and to refuse sanction. Those are different targets, and the ladder shows that they belong to different rungs. **Second, it shows the direction of change**, which an abstract argument cannot: the appellate forum was moved to a constitutional court in 2021, the executive's revisional power over certification was removed in 2023, and the age markers were made explicitly advisory - three movements toward narrowing discretionary control - while the same 2023 statute extended the regime across media and created new offences. **Third, it disciplines the argument with dates**, so that a candidate does not attack a tribunal that no longer exists or defend a category that has been replaced. The defensible position is therefore that the normative question is real and is answered best by someone who knows precisely which powers currently exist; the ladder is not a substitute for the argument but its precondition.

### Examination use

This lesson is the **currentness** lesson of the topic and its value in an answer is disproportionate, because very few candidates can state the present position accurately. Use the ladder to structure any question on the evolution of film regulation; use the **currentness panel** to avoid the four dead statements; and use the **two-directions observation** about the 2023 amendment - narrowing discretionary control while extending reach and creating offences - as the analytical close. In an objective item, the highest-probability targets are the **omission of the appellate tribunal**, the **High Court appeal route**, the **three UA age markers**, the **advisory character of the age endorsement**, the **perpetual validity** of certificates, and the fact that **U, A and S were retained**.

### Traps introduced here

- Describing the Film Certification Appellate Tribunal as the current appeal forum; section **5D was omitted** in 2021 and appeals lie to the **High Court** under section **5C**.
- Attributing the tribunal's removal to the film statute rather than to the **Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021**.
- Stating the old UA description - parental guidance for children under 12 - as current.
- Saying that U, A or S were replaced; only **UA** was re-cut.
- Treating the age markers as enforceable restrictions rather than as endorsements **informing parents or guardians**.
- Saying that certificates lapse after ten years; they are now **perpetually valid**.
- Asserting section numbers for the piracy offences, or a notification or rule-making date, none of which is established here.

### Revision notes

1. **1927** - **Indian Cinematograph Committee**: a state inquiry, not a board.
2. **1950** - the **Central Board of Film Censors** is set up, **before** the Act.
3. **1952** - the **Cinematograph Act** constitutes the Board and **renames** it the Central Board of Film **Certification**. Categories: **U** and **A**.
4. **1983** - **Cinematograph (Certification) Rules** upgradation adds **UA** (parental guidance, children under 12) and **S** (specialised audience such as doctors, engineers). Made through **rules**, not a statutory amendment.
5. **UA** solved the intermediate case on the **age** axis; **S** solved the **occupation** axis. Different axes, different categories.
6. **1995** - **Cable Television Network (Regulation) Act**: a **separate, lighter** regime for cable content; offences **non-cognizable**.
7. **2021** - **Tribunals Reforms Act** omits section **5D**, which had constituted the **Film Certification Appellate Tribunal**, with retrospective effect from **4 April 2021**.
8. The current appeal route is section **5C**, to the **High Court** - a constitutional court replacing a specialised tribunal.
9. **2023** - the **Cinematograph (Amendment) Act** replaces **UA** with **UA 7+**, **UA 13+** and **UA 16+**, and **retains U, A and S**.
10. The age endorsement **informs the guidance of parents or guardians** and is **not enforceable by any other person**.
11. The amendment also requires a **separate certificate** for exhibiting an **'A' or 'S'** film on television or other prescribed media.
12. It creates offences of **unauthorised recording** and **unauthorised exhibition**, including **attempt**, with **Copyright Act, 1957** exemptions for private or personal use, reporting of current affairs and review or critique; punishment is **three months to three years** and a fine of **three lakh rupees to five per cent of audited gross production cost**.
13. Certificates move from **ten-year** validity to **perpetual** validity, and the central government's **revisional power** over certification is **removed**.
14. The amendment runs in **two directions at once**: narrowing discretionary control, while extending reach across media and creating new offences.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Trace the evolution of India's film certification framework and assess the direction of recent change. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The framework is a ladder, each rung adding or removing a power.

**Origins.** The **Indian Cinematograph Committee** of **1927**, examining the **adequacy of censorship** and the **"immoral effect"** of films, shows State concern preceding independence. The **Central Board of Film Censors** was set up in **1950**; the **Cinematograph Act, 1952**, instituted **to certify films**, constituted and **renamed** it the Central Board of Film **Certification**. Categories: **U** and **A**.

**Subdivision.** The **1983** upgradation of the **Cinematograph (Certification) Rules** added **UA** - parental guidance under 12 - and **S**, for a specialised audience: UA the **age** axis, S the **occupational** axis.

**A parallel regime.** The **Cable Television Network (Regulation) Act, 1995** created a lighter code for cable content, with **non-cognizable** offences, against the film statute's **cognisable and non-bailable** one.

**Recent change, two directions.** In **2021** the **Tribunals Reforms Act** omitted section **5D**, ending the **Film Certification Appellate Tribunal** retrospectively from **4 April 2021**; appeals now lie under section **5C** to the **High Court**. The **Cinematograph (Amendment) Act, 2023** replaced **UA** with **UA 7+, UA 13+, UA 16+**, retained **U, A and S**, made the age endorsement **advisory to parents or guardians**, certificates **perpetually valid**, and **removed the central government's revisional power** - all narrowing discretion. Simultaneously it extended the regime: a **separate certificate** for 'A' and 'S' films on television or prescribed media, and offences of **unauthorised recording and exhibition**.

**Assessment.** The direction is not liberalisation but **re-specification**: less executive discretion over individual films, more precise audience information, a wider enforcement perimeter.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the ladder given with at least six correctly dated rungs 6 + the 1983 additions explained as two different axes rather than two more categories 3 + the 1995 regime correctly placed as parallel rather than as film certification, with the offence asymmetry 2 + the 2021 omission with its instrument, its retrospective date and the new High Court route 4 + the 2023 changes with the three age markers, retention of U, A and S, advisory character, perpetual validity and removal of the revisional power 4 + a two-direction assessment rather than a single verdict 1.** Describing the appellate tribunal as current forfeits four marks, because it is the single most important dead statement in this topic.

### Concept check

**Question:** A coaching note states: "Under the Cinematograph Act, films are certified U, A, UA or S; a UA certificate means parental guidance for children under 12; an aggrieved producer may appeal to the Film Certification Appellate Tribunal; and certificates must be renewed every ten years." Identify each outdated statement and give the current position with its instrument.

**Model answer:** **All four statements were once accurate and three are now wrong; the first is partly wrong.** **On categories:** the note's list is the position established by the **1952** Act as supplemented by the **1983** upgradation of the **Cinematograph (Certification) Rules**, which added **UA** and **S** to the existing **U** and **A**. The current position, under the **Cinematograph (Amendment) Act, 2023**, **retains U, A and S** but replaces the single **UA** with three age-marked categories - **UA 7+**, **UA 13+** and **UA 16+**. **On the meaning of UA:** "parental guidance for children under the age of 12 years" was the 1983 formulation. Under the current scheme the age endorsement within the UA category exists to **inform the guidance of parents or guardians** and is **not enforceable by any person other than parents or guardians** - so it is an advisory marker, not an enforceable age bar. **On appeal:** the **Film Certification Appellate Tribunal** was constituted under section **5D** of the Act. Section 5D was **omitted by the Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021**, with retrospective effect from **4 April 2021**. The current route is section **5C**, which permits an appeal to the **High Court** - and note that the abolition came through a general tribunals-reform statute, not through a film-policy decision. **On validity:** certificates were formerly valid for **ten years**; under the 2023 amendment they are **perpetually valid**, so the renewal requirement has gone. **A fifth change the note omits** is that the same amendment **removed the central government's revisional power** over films certified or pending certification, and required a **separate certificate** for exhibiting an 'A' or 'S' film on television or other prescribed media.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that a published note on film law remains accurate because the underlying Act is old. The Act of 1952 is still the governing statute, but its categories, its appellate provision, its certificate validity and its ministerial oversight have all been altered by later instruments - and the instrument that made each change is itself examinable.

## Lesson 12 - The institutional map by function, and what changed when four bodies became one

Progress: 12/16 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: certification, training, archiving, development and production, documentary and children's film, and festivals - the six jobs; the founding facts for each body; the 23 December 2020 consolidation decision and its consequences; and the currentness caution that governs every institutional sentence in this topic

The lower panel is the reason this lesson exists. The four merged names are printed everywhere and are still perfectly good *historical* facts; they are no longer accurate as descriptions of who acts today. The discipline is to keep the founding fact and the current arrangement in separate sentences.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Institutional questions look like memory work and are actually classification work. A candidate who has memorised six acronyms in a list cannot answer "discuss India's film institutions", because a list is not an argument and, worse, the list is partly out of date.

The organising principle that fixes both problems is **function**. There are six jobs to be done in a film ecosystem - certify, train, archive, develop, produce documentary and children's film, and exhibit and reward - and India created separate bodies for each. Once the six jobs are the skeleton, the bodies hang off them, the consolidation becomes a comprehensible administrative event rather than a confusing list of mergers, and the evaluative question ("was the consolidation wise?") becomes answerable.

### 2. The six jobs, and why each needed its own body

**Certify.** A legal precondition for exhibition must be administered by a body with statutory powers, procedures and grounds. It is a **regulatory** job, and it cannot be combined with production without an obvious conflict.

**Train.** An industry needs a reproducible supply of directors, cinematographers, editors and sound designers. This is an **educational** job, and it is the job whose absence is least visible and most damaging over time.

**Archive.** Film decays. Somebody must collect, catalogue, store and restore it. This is a **custodial** job on a timescale far longer than any production cycle, and it has no commercial constituency, which is precisely why it must be public.

**Develop and produce.** Certain films - by subject, by language, by scale - will not be financed commercially. A public corporation that finances, promotes and takes them to markets performs a **developmental** job.

**Produce documentary and children's film.** These are two specific kinds of film with weak commercial logic and strong public rationale, and India created a dedicated body for each.

**Exhibit and reward.** Festivals programme; awards recognise. Both are **public-facing** jobs and neither confers any legal status on a film.

**The argument that earns the mark:** these six jobs have different time-horizons, different constituencies and different conflict risks. An archive's logic is preservation over decades; a development corporation's logic is projects and returns; a regulator's logic is compliance. That is why they were separate in the first place - and it is also the strongest available reservation about merging four of them, as section 5 sets out.

### 3. The bodies, with their founding facts

**Certification - the Central Board of Film Certification.** Set up in **1950** as the **Central Board of Film Censors**, renamed under the **Cinematograph Act, 1952**, **directly under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting**, head office **Mumbai** with nine regional offices and a regional facilitation office at Chandigarh. Full treatment in Lesson 10.

**Training - the Film and Television Institute of India, Pune.** Set up by the Government of India in **1960** as the **Film Institute of India**, under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting. Following the addition of the **Television Wing in 1971** the Institute was **re-designated** the Film and Television Institute of India. It was **registered as a Society in October 1974** under the **Societies Registration Act, 1860**. It is governed by a **Governing Council headed by a Chairman**, with an **Academic Council** for academic policy and a **Standing Finance Committee** for financial matters, and it consists of two wings, **Film** and **Television**, offering post-graduate diploma programmes in the film and television crafts.

Note the three-step institutional history, because it is the kind of detail that distinguishes a precise answer: **founded 1960 under a different name**, **expanded and re-designated after 1971**, **registered as a society in 1974**. A candidate who writes only "FTII, established 1960" is not wrong, but a candidate who can give the three steps has an identifiably better sentence.

**Training - the Satyajit Ray Film and Television Institute, Kolkata.** Established by the Government of India in **1995** as an **autonomous educational institution** under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting, **registered under the West Bengal Societies Registration Act, 1961**, located at **Kolkata** and **named after Satyajit Ray**. It provides higher and professional education in film-making and television production, offering post-graduate programmes in film specialisations and a post-graduate diploma programme in electronic and digital media.

Two points of contrast with the Pune institute are worth a sentence each. The registration statutes differ - a **central** societies statute of 1860 for one, a **State** societies statute of 1961 for the other - which is a small but genuine institutional distinction. And the naming is itself the topic speaking to itself: a national training institution named for a figure of the parallel tradition of Lesson 7.

**Archiving - the National Film Archive of India.** Established in **1964** as a unit under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting, at **Pune**, for **film preservation**. Its functions now sit within the consolidated arrangement described in section 4. Lesson 14 treats preservation as a practice.

**Development and production - the National Film Development Corporation.** Set up by the Government of India in **1975**, with the primary objective of **planning, promoting and organising an integrated and efficient development of the Indian film industry** in accordance with national economic policy and the objectives laid down by the Central Government. In **1980**, the **Film Finance Corporation** and the **Indian Motion Picture Export Corporation** were **merged with NFDC**.

The 1980 merger is worth noticing on its own, quite apart from the later one. It tells you that NFDC's mandate has been assembled by absorption from the beginning: a **financing** body and an **export** body were folded into a development corporation within five years of its creation. The 2020-22 consolidation is therefore the second and larger instance of an established administrative pattern, not an aberration.

**Documentary and children's film - the Films Division and the Children's Film Society, India.** These are the public documentary body and the children's film body respectively. Their founding years are commonly given as **1948** for the Films Division and **1955** for the Children's Film Society; this session records those years with the status attached to them in the ledger, because they were corroborated from secondary material rather than confirmed from an official page in this pass. What is confirmed from official material is that both bodies existed and that **their functions were merged into NFDC**.

**Festivals - the Directorate of Film Festivals.** Set up by the Government of India in **1973** for the express purpose of **organising national and international film festivals in India**, so that Indian films find recognition at global platforms and foreign films are showcased to Indian audiences. It was **also given the additional charge of organising the National Film Awards functions** - a fact that matters greatly in Lesson 13, because it explains why the printed literature attributes the national awards to this body.

**The festival itself - the International Film Festival of India.** Established in **1952**, IFFI is **Asia's oldest and pre-eminent international film festival** and is recognised as a **Competitive Film Festival** by the **International Federation of Film Producers' Associations**. It is **organised by NFDC, under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting, in association with the Government of Goa and the Entertainment Society of Goa**.

Note the two things that sentence establishes: IFFI's **status** as a competitive festival accredited by an international federation - which matters because Lesson 13 distinguishes competitive festivals from non-competitive showcases - and its **current organiser**, which is NFDC and not the directorate that printed sources name.

### 4. The consolidation: what was decided, and when

Pursuant to the decision of the Government of India dated **23 December 2020** to merge four film media units - the **Films Division**, the **Directorate of Film Festivals**, the **National Film Archive of India** and the **Children's Film Society, India** - with the **National Film Development Corporation of India**, **all functions of those four bodies are merged into NFDC**. NFDC is described in official material as now being a fully integrated film development corporation, positioned to facilitate development, production, promotion and the preservation of India's film heritage across Indian languages.

Four disciplines attach to that paragraph and they are the whole examinable content of the consolidation.

**Count and name them exactly: four units, merged into a fifth.** The four are Films Division, the Directorate of Film Festivals, the National Film Archive of India and the Children's Film Society, India. NFDC is the receiving body and is not one of the four. An option that includes NFDC among the merged units, or that includes the CBFC or a training institute, is wrong - the **regulator and the training institutes were not part of the consolidation**.

**Date the decision, not the outcome.** The **decision is dated 23 December 2020**; the functional integration followed. This session dates the **decision** precisely and does not assert a completion date for the integration.

**Say "consolidated", not "created".** NFDC existed from **1975**. Its mandate **widened by administrative absorption**, not by a new statute. An answer that describes NFDC as having been created to take over these functions has the sequence backwards.

**Keep the founding fact and the current arrangement in separate sentences.** The safe formulation for the archive is: *film preservation was institutionally housed in the National Film Archive of India, established in 1964, and now sits within the consolidated NFDC architecture.* The same two-clause form works for the festivals directorate and for the awards, and it is what protects an answer from being simultaneously out of date and un-historical.

### 5. Comparative treatment: the case for the consolidation and the case against

| | The case for | The case against |
|---|---|---|
| **Resources** | one corporation, shared overheads, no duplicated administration | savings in administration are not the binding constraint on archiving or children's film |
| **Coherence** | development, documentary, festivals, archive and children's film all serve one ecosystem; a single body can plan across them | they serve it on **different timescales** - an archive plans in decades, a production body in projects |
| **Visibility** | one recognisable public film body rather than five with overlapping names | the specialist mandate is easier to defend when it has its own institution and its own budget line |
| **Specialist logic** | integration can route resources to whichever function needs them | an archive's preservation logic is not a production corporation's logic, and the weaker mandates are the ones at risk when priorities compete |

**How to write this.** The stated rationale is administrative and resource-related, and that should be reported as the rationale rather than endorsed. The risk - loss of specialist mandates - should be stated as a **risk** and not asserted as an outcome, because this session holds no evidence about what has actually happened to any of the four functions since the merger. A well-marked sentence looks like this: *the consolidation was directed to integrate five bodies serving one ecosystem into a single corporation; whether it preserves the distinct logics of archiving and children's film, whose timescales and constituencies differ from a development corporation's, is a question the decision itself does not settle.*

### 6. What this session does not claim about any of these institutions

Stated openly, because the temptation to embellish institutional answers is strong and the cost is high.

No intake numbers, admission processes, fee structures, campus histories, notable alumni, heads or directors, controversies, protests or disputes are asserted for either training institute. No holdings, reel counts, restoration project names, budgets or acquisition figures are asserted for the archive. No film titles, production slates, co-production arrangements, festival line-ups, delegate numbers or market statistics are asserted for the development corporation or the festival. No founding year is asserted for any body beyond those given above, and the two years given with a corroboration caveat are flagged as such. No completion date for the merger's implementation is asserted, and no claim is made about the effect of the merger on any function.

### 7. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** Function is a tidy organising principle, but it is a rationalisation after the fact. These bodies were created at different times for different political reasons across more than seven decades, and presenting them as a designed six-job architecture imposes a coherence that never existed. The consolidation of 2020 is itself evidence: if the design had been coherent, it would not have needed rationalising.

**Reply.** The historical point is correct and should be conceded without hedging: no one designed this architecture in advance. The bodies accumulated - a board in 1950, a festival in 1952, a training institute in 1960, an archive in 1964, a festivals directorate in 1973, a development corporation in 1975 with two further bodies folded into it by 1980, a second training institute in 1995. That is accretion, not design. But the functional frame survives the objection for two reasons. **First, it is descriptively true regardless of intention**: the jobs are genuinely different, they were in fact performed by different bodies, and the differences between them are real differences of timescale, constituency and conflict risk rather than labels imposed by an analyst. **Second, the consolidation supports the frame rather than undermining it.** What was merged was exactly the set of bodies that do **not** carry a regulatory or educational function: the regulator and both training institutes were left outside. That pattern is only explicable if the functional distinctions are doing real work - you cannot fold a certifying authority into a production corporation, and the decision did not attempt it. The defensible position is therefore that the architecture is accreted in origin and functional in structure, and that the second fact is what makes it analysable at all.

### Examination use

Any question on India's film institutions, on public support for cinema, or on film preservation's institutional home is answered from this lesson. The winning structure is **six jobs first, bodies second, consolidation third, evaluation fourth** - which converts a list into an argument. The **two-clause formulation** in section 4 is the single most useful sentence-shape in this lesson, because it keeps an answer both current and historically accurate. In an objective item, the recurring targets are the **four merged units and their identity**, the **23 December 2020** decision date, **NFDC's 1975 founding and its 1980 absorptions**, **FTII's 1960 founding under a different name and its 1974 society registration**, **SRFTI's 1995 founding and its State registration statute**, **NFAI's 1964 founding at Pune**, **DFF's 1973 founding and its additional awards charge**, and **IFFI's 1952 establishment and competitive accreditation**.

### Traps introduced here

- Including NFDC, the CBFC or a training institute among the four merged units.
- Saying that NFDC was **created** by the merger; it was set up in **1975** and **widened** by absorption.
- Attributing the organisation of IFFI to the Directorate of Film Festivals today; it is organised by **NFDC** with the Government of Goa and the Entertainment Society of Goa.
- Writing that FTII was founded under that name; it was set up in **1960** as the **Film Institute of India**.
- Confusing the two training institutes' registration statutes - **central** 1860 statute for Pune, **West Bengal** 1961 statute for Kolkata.
- Asserting alumni, heads, intakes, holdings, budgets or controversies for any of these bodies.
- Asserting that the consolidation has damaged or improved any function; the risk is real, the outcome is not established here.

### Revision notes

1. **Six jobs:** certify, train, archive, develop and produce, make documentary and children's film, exhibit and reward. Different timescales, constituencies and conflict risks.
2. **CBFC** - certification; set up **1950** as the Board of Film **Censors**, renamed under the **1952** Act; under **MIB**; head office **Mumbai**.
3. **FTII, Pune** - training; set up **1960** as the **Film Institute of India**; **Television Wing 1971** and re-designation; **registered as a Society in October 1974** under the **Societies Registration Act, 1860**; Governing Council under a Chairman, Academic Council, Standing Finance Committee; **Film and Television** wings.
4. **SRFTI, Kolkata** - training; established **1995** as an autonomous educational institution under **MIB**; registered under the **West Bengal Societies Registration Act, 1961**; **named after Satyajit Ray**.
5. **NFAI, Pune** - archiving; established **1964** under **MIB** for **film preservation**.
6. **NFDC** - development and production; set up **1975**; in **1980** the **Film Finance Corporation** and the **Indian Motion Picture Export Corporation** were merged into it.
7. **Films Division** - public documentary body; **Children's Film Society, India** - children's film body. Founding years commonly given as **1948** and **1955**, recorded here with a corroboration caveat.
8. **Directorate of Film Festivals** - set up **1973** to organise national and international film festivals in India, and **also** given charge of organising the **National Film Awards** functions.
9. **IFFI** - established **1952**; **Asia's oldest and pre-eminent** international film festival; recognised as a **Competitive Film Festival** by the international federation of film producers' associations; **organised by NFDC** under **MIB** with the **Government of Goa** and the **Entertainment Society of Goa**.
10. **The consolidation:** Government of India decision dated **23 December 2020** to merge **four** units - **Films Division, Directorate of Film Festivals, National Film Archive of India, Children's Film Society, India** - with **NFDC**. All their functions are merged into NFDC.
11. The **regulator and both training institutes were not merged**, which is itself evidence that the functional distinctions are real.
12. Say **consolidated, not created**: NFDC's mandate widened by **administrative absorption**, not by statute.
13. The **two-clause discipline**: give the founding fact, then the current arrangement, in separate clauses.
14. The **evaluative line**: the stated rationale is integration and resource use; the stated risk is the loss of specialist mandates whose logics and timescales differ. Report both; assert neither outcome.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Discuss the institutional architecture supporting Indian cinema, and evaluate the recent consolidation of film media units. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

Read the architecture by **function**: separate bodies, separate jobs.

**Certification.** The **Central Board of Film Certification**, set up **1950** as the Board of Film **Censors**, renamed under the **Cinematograph Act, 1952**, is a statutory regulator under the **Ministry of Information and Broadcasting**.

**Training.** The **Film and Television Institute of India, Pune**, set up **1960** as the **Film Institute of India**, added a Television Wing in **1971**, becoming a **Society in October 1974** under the **Societies Registration Act, 1860**. The **Satyajit Ray Film and Television Institute, Kolkata**, followed in **1995** under the **West Bengal Societies Registration Act, 1961**.

**Archiving.** The **National Film Archive of India**, **Pune**, **1964**, housed film preservation.

**Development.** The **National Film Development Corporation**, **1975**, absorbed the **Film Finance Corporation** and the **Indian Motion Picture Export Corporation** in **1980**.

**Documentary, children's film, festivals.** The **Films Division**, the **Children's Film Society, India**, and the **Directorate of Film Festivals** - **1973**, also charged with the **National Film Awards** functions - completed it. **IFFI**, **1952**, is Asia's oldest and a **competitive** festival.

**The consolidation.** By a Government decision dated **23 December 2020**, four units - **Films Division**, **Directorate of Film Festivals**, **National Film Archive of India**, **Children's Film Society, India** - merged with **NFDC**. The regulator and both training institutes stayed outside.

**Evaluation.** The rationale is integration and resource use. The risk: an **archive plans in decades**, a production corporation in projects, so weaker mandates may lose their budget line. NFDC was **consolidated, not created**; whether the merged logics survive is unsettled.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the six functions used as the organising spine rather than a list of acronyms 3 + at least six bodies with correct founding years 5 + the consolidation given with its exact decision date and all four merged units correctly named 3 + the observation that the regulator and training institutes were excluded 2 + a balanced evaluation that reports the rationale and states the risk without asserting an outcome 2.** Writing that NFDC was created by the merger, or naming NFDC as one of the merged units, forfeits three marks each time, because both errors misdescribe the direction of the administrative change.

### Concept check

**Question:** A press note reads: "The Directorate of Film Festivals, which runs IFFI and the National Film Awards, will work with the National Film Archive of India to preserve classic cinema." Identify what is historically accurate in this sentence, what is no longer accurate, and how each clause should be rewritten.

**Model answer:** **What is historically accurate.** The **Directorate of Film Festivals** was indeed set up by the Government of India in **1973** for the express purpose of organising **national and international film festivals in India**, and it was **also given the additional charge of organising the National Film Awards functions**. The **National Film Archive of India** was indeed **established in 1964** under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting at **Pune** for **film preservation**. Every institutional attribution in the sentence was once correct. **What is no longer accurate.** Pursuant to a Government of India decision dated **23 December 2020**, four film media units - the **Films Division**, the **Directorate of Film Festivals**, the **National Film Archive of India** and the **Children's Film Society, India** - were merged with the **National Film Development Corporation**, and all their functions are now merged into NFDC. The sentence therefore describes two bodies acting in their own names and collaborating with each other, when their functions now sit inside a single corporation. Separately, **IFFI** - established **1952**, Asia's oldest and pre-eminent international film festival and a **competitive** festival - is **organised by NFDC** under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting, in association with the **Government of Goa** and the **Entertainment Society of Goa**. **How to rewrite.** Use the two-clause form throughout: *the organisation of national and international film festivals in India, including IFFI, and of the National Film Awards functions, was housed in the Directorate of Film Festivals, established 1973, and now sits within NFDC; film preservation was housed in the National Film Archive of India, established 1964 at Pune, and likewise now sits within NFDC, which was set up in 1975 and whose mandate was widened by the 2020 consolidation rather than created by it.*

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a merger erases the founding facts. It does not - the founding years, purposes and locations remain correct history and remain examinable. What changes is the **present tense**: which body acts today. Keeping the two in separate clauses is the discipline that lets an answer be accurate about both.

## Lesson 13 - Awards, festivals and the hierarchy of recognitions

Progress: 13/16 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the National Film Awards and the certification gate that defines eligibility; the Dadasaheb Phalke Award with its institution year, its composition and its first recipient; the award-year against ceremony-year discipline demonstrated on two consecutive editions; festivals and the selection-nomination-victory ladder; and the separateness of the international award systems

CA found: The 72nd National Film Awards were conferred by the President of India at Ekta Nagar, Kevadia, Gujarat, on 22 September 2026, together with the Dadasaheb Phalke Award for award year 2024 - checked 29 September 2026. This is the single current linkage claimed anywhere in this session
