### Visual: the recognition ladder, and what each rung actually is

```text
 =============== WHAT KIND OF RECOGNITION IS IT? ===============

  LEVEL 4  THE HIGHEST INDIVIDUAL HONOUR IN INDIAN CINEMA
           DADASAHEB PHALKE AWARD - instituted 1969
             for outstanding contribution to the GROWTH AND
             DEVELOPMENT of Indian cinema
             selected by a Committee of eminent personalities
               from the Indian film industry
             comprises a SWARNA KAMAL (Golden Lotus) medallion
               and a cash prize of Rs 10 lakh
             commemorates Dadasaheb Phalke (1870-1944), maker of
               Raja Harishchandra (1913)
             first recipient: DEVIKA RANI, 1969
                          |
  LEVEL 3  NATIONAL RECOGNITION FOR WORKS AND CRAFTS
           NATIONAL FILM AWARDS - across FEATURE, NON-FEATURE and
             CINEMA-WRITING categories
             ELIGIBILITY GATE: films CERTIFIED BY THE CBFC within
               the stated calendar year - so certification, a
               regulatory act, is the precondition for an award
                          |
  LEVEL 2  A COMPETITIVE FESTIVAL PRIZE
           IFFI - established 1952; Asia's oldest and pre-eminent
             international film festival; recognised as a
             COMPETITIVE film festival by the international
             federation of film producers' associations
                          |
  LEVEL 1  FESTIVAL SELECTION / PROGRAMMING
           the film has been INVITED TO SCREEN
           this is NOT a nomination and NOT a prize

 =============== THE THREE-STEP LADDER, SEPARATELY ==============
   SELECTED  ->  NOMINATED  ->  AWARDED
   programmed    shortlisted    won, in a NAMED CATEGORY,
   to screen     in a specific  in a NAMED YEAR, by a NAMED
                 category       institution's jury
   Each arrow is a distinct achievement. Skipping an arrow in a
   sentence is the commonest award error in this topic.

 =============== THE SEPARATE INTERNATIONAL SYSTEMS =============
   BAFTA   = the awards of the BRITISH ACADEMY OF FILM AND
             TELEVISION ARTS
   ACADEMY = the awards of the US ACADEMY OF MOTION PICTURE
     AWARDS  ARTS AND SCIENCES
   CANNES / VENICE / BERLIN = prizes awarded WITHIN distinct
             international film FESTIVALS
   These are three different kinds of body. They are not
   interchangeable, and a prize in one is not a prize in another.
```

The ladder is the lesson's structure and the three-step arrow is its discipline. Almost every wrong award sentence is produced by moving up the ladder without earning the rung, or by treating two of the international systems as one.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Awards feel like the easiest material in this topic and are the most dangerous, because the natural way to talk about them is exactly the way that loses marks. "It won at Cannes", "it got an Oscar nomination", "it was India's entry" and "it won a BAFTA" are all sentences that leave out at least one of the three qualifiers that make an award claim checkable: **the institution**, **the category** and **the year**.

There is also a second, subtler problem, and it is the one that the current material in this lesson demonstrates: **award year and ceremony year are different**, and the difference is not small. An award can be *for* one year, *announced* in a second and *conferred* in a third. A candidate who collapses those into one date will be wrong even when every name is right.

### 2. The National Film Awards, and the gate that defines them

The National Film Awards are India's national recognition across **feature**, **non-feature** and **cinema-writing** categories.

The institutional history has to be stated in the two-clause form that Lesson 12 established. The **Directorate of Film Festivals**, set up in **1973** to organise national and international film festivals in India, was **also given the additional charge of organising the National Film Awards functions** - which is why printed literature attributes the awards to that body. The Directorate was one of the four units merged with **NFDC** by the Government decision dated **23 December 2020**, so the awards are today administered within the **Ministry of Information and Broadcasting's** framework, with the merged corporation carrying the operational functions, and are conferred by the **President of India**.

**The eligibility gate is the most interesting institutional fact here and it is almost never used.** Eligibility for a given edition runs to films **certified by the Central Board of Film Certification within the relevant calendar year**. Read what that means: a **regulatory** act - certification - is the **precondition** for consideration for a **recognition**. The two acts that Lesson 1 insisted on keeping separate are connected here by an administrative rule, and the connection is one of sequence, not of nature. Certification does not make a film award-worthy; it makes the film *eligible to be considered*. A candidate who can state that relation precisely has the cleanest possible demonstration that they understand the difference between the two acts.

**What this session does not assert about the National Film Awards.** No year of institution is claimed, because none was established from an official source in this pass. No complete list of categories is given. No winners other than those recorded in section 4 are named, and no category-by-category results are claimed for any edition.

### 3. The Dadasaheb Phalke Award, stated in full

This is the most densely examinable single entry in the topic, and every element of it is sourceable.

- **Instituted in 1969.**
- It is **India's highest award in cinema**.
- It **commemorates Dadasaheb Phalke (1870-1944)**, the film-maker who made **India's first full-length feature film, *Raja Harishchandra*, in 1913**.
- It is given for **outstanding contribution to the growth and development of Indian cinema**.
- It is **selected by a Committee consisting of eminent personalities from the Indian film industry**.
- It comprises a **Swarna Kamal - Golden Lotus - medallion** and a **cash prize of ten lakh rupees**.
- The **first recipient was Devika Rani, in 1969**.
- The printed source names the **Directorate of Film Festivals** as the awarding body, that Directorate being an organisation set up under the **Ministry of Information and Broadcasting**.

Four observations that convert this list into answer material.

**The award is for a body of contribution, not for a work.** "Growth and development of Indian cinema" is a career and field-level criterion, which is why it is conferred on a person rather than on a film and why it sits above the work-level National Film Award categories rather than among them.

**The selection body is drawn from the industry.** This is a genuine feature: the highest state honour in Indian cinema is decided by a committee of film people, not by officials. It is the strongest available evidence for the claim that the award is a peer recognition administered by the State rather than a State judgement on cinema.

**The awarding body named in print has been merged.** The Directorate of Film Festivals was one of the four units consolidated into NFDC by the 23 December 2020 decision. The two-clause discipline therefore applies here as it does everywhere in this topic: *the award is instituted in 1969 and was administered by the Directorate of Film Festivals; that Directorate's functions now sit within NFDC, and the award is conferred by the President of India under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting's national film awards framework.*

**The 1913 link closes a circle in this session.** The award commemorates the maker of the first indigenous Indian feature of Lesson 3, and the awards chapter's phrasing - **India's first full-length feature film** - differs slightly from the cinema chapter's **first indigenous Indian silent film**. Lesson 3 recorded that variance; it is recorded again here rather than resolved.

### 4. Award year against ceremony year: two consecutive editions, demonstrated

This is the section that makes the discipline concrete, and it uses the session's single current linkage.

**The 71st National Film Awards.** The latest official Dadasaheb Phalke announcement recorded in the canonical material for this topic, rechecked on **4 September 2026**, names **Mohanlal** for **award year 2023**, at the **71st** National Film Awards. The ceremony for that edition took place on **23 September 2025**.

**The 72nd National Film Awards - the current linkage.** The winners of the **72nd** National Film Awards were announced on **18 July 2026** by the **National Film Award Cell** of the **Ministry of Information and Broadcasting**, for films of the **2024** award year, eligibility running to films **certified by the Central Board of Film Certification between 1 January 2024 and 31 December 2024**. The awards were conferred by the **President of India, Droupadi Murmu**, on **22 September 2026** at **Ekta Nagar, Kevadia, in Gujarat**, together with the **Dadasaheb Phalke Award for award year 2024**, conferred on the veteran actor **Anant Nag**. Checked **29 September 2026**.

**The provenance, stated rather than implied.** The ceremony's date and venue are taken from the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting's own press-release record; the conferral of the Dadasaheb Phalke Award at that ceremony is recorded in the President's Secretariat's press-release record; the recipient's name is recorded from that release and from contemporaneous national reporting, and the full text of the Secretariat's release could not be opened directly in this pass, so the name is carried with that stated status. No category-by-category list of the edition's other winners is asserted anywhere in this session.

**Now put the two editions side by side**, because the pattern is the teachable thing:

| | 71st edition | 72nd edition |
|---|---|---|
| Award year | **2023** | **2024** |
| Announced | not recorded here | **18 July 2026** |
| Conferred at a ceremony on | **23 September 2025** | **22 September 2026** |
| Dadasaheb Phalke Award recipient | **Mohanlal** | **Anant Nag** |
| Gap between award year and ceremony | about two years | about two years |

**Three rules fall straight out of the table.**

**Never equate award year with ceremony year.** In both editions the gap is roughly two years. A sentence such as "the 2026 Dadasaheb Phalke Award" is ambiguous at best and wrong at worst: 2026 is the year of a ceremony at which an award for **2024** was conferred.

**Never assume the newest recipient you have heard of is current.** The canonical record for this topic, verified in early September 2026, named the **2023** recipient. Within three weeks the **2024** award had been conferred on a different person. Any award fact in this topic is a photograph with a date on it.

**Never name a recipient from memory.** Both names here are carried with their source and their date. The moment a candidate writes a recipient without a date, the claim is unverifiable, and recipients change annually.

### 5. Festivals, and the three-step ladder

**IFFI** was **established in 1952**, is **Asia's oldest and pre-eminent international film festival**, and is recognised as a **Competitive Film Festival** by the **International Federation of Film Producers' Associations**. It is **organised by NFDC under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting, in association with the Government of Goa and the Entertainment Society of Goa**.

The word **competitive** is doing real work there. A competitive festival awards prizes; a showcase festival programmes films without awarding them. So a festival is not automatically an award-giving body, and a film screened at one is not thereby in contention for anything.

That yields the ladder:

**Selected.** The festival has programmed the film. This is an invitation to screen. It is a genuine achievement - programming is competitive - but it confers no prize and no nomination.

**Nominated.** The film or a person has been shortlisted **in a specific category** of a specific award. Note that the category is part of the fact: there is no such thing as a general nomination.

**Awarded.** A jury of a named institution has chosen a winner **in a named category** **in a named year**.

Each arrow is separately earned, and a sentence that skips one is making a claim the speaker cannot support. This is the single most common inflation in casual talk about Indian films abroad, and it is exactly what a statement-elimination item is built to catch.

### 6. The international systems, kept apart

| System | What it is | What it is **not** |
|---|---|---|
| **BAFTA Film Awards** | the awards of the **British Academy of Film and Television Arts** | not the Academy Awards, not a festival prize |
| **Academy Awards** | the awards of the **US Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences** | not BAFTA, not a festival prize |
| **Cannes, Venice, Berlin prizes** | prizes awarded **within** distinct international film festivals | not academy awards of any country |
| **Festival selection or premiere** | the film is programmed or screened | not a nomination and not a win |

Three different kinds of body are involved - a British academy, an American academy, and festivals - and they have different memberships, different procedures and different categories. An answer that treats "international award" as a single currency has erased those differences, and an objective item that offers BAFTA and the Academy Awards as the same institution is testing exactly this.

**The worked instance this session holds.** The official 2026 BAFTA result lists ***Boong*** as the winner in the **Children's and Family Film** category, and the film was **directed by Lakshmipriya Devi**. Three disciplines attach, and they are the model for every award sentence a candidate will ever write.

- **Keep the exact category.** "Won a BAFTA" is too broad to be useful, because a statement-elimination item can test the category independently of the award.
- **Keep the credit exact.** The recorded fact is a **direction** credit for a named person. It is not a production, writing or acting credit.
- **Tie any "first" to the exact category and a dated source.** A claim that a film is the *first Indian film* to do something must specify *the first to do what, in which category, on whose record*. It must not be expanded into "the first Indian film ever to receive any recognition from that academy", which is a different and much larger claim.

**The analytical use.** The enrichment reading of this result is that international recognition of such a film can illustrate the visibility of **regional and Northeast Indian storytelling**, and that the analytical point is not that an award was won but that **global circulation can widen the cultural map through which Indian cinema is perceived**. That is offered as an analytical reading, clearly marked as such, and it is separate from the three verified facts above.

### 7. Comparative treatment: what each recognition does and does not do

| Recognition | Who decides | What it changes | What it does **not** do |
|---|---|---|---|
| **CBFC certificate** | a statutory Board | makes public exhibition lawful for a defined audience | says nothing about merit; is not an award |
| **Festival selection** | a festival's programmers | brings the film before a particular audience and press | confers no prize and no nomination |
| **Competitive festival prize** | that festival's jury | recognition by that festival, in that category, that year | does not transfer to any other festival or academy |
| **National Film Award** | a national jury under the MIB framework | national recognition of a work or craft | does not confer any legal status on the film |
| **Dadasaheb Phalke Award** | a committee of eminent film-industry personalities | India's highest recognition of a **person's** contribution to the growth and development of Indian cinema | is not a category of the National Film Awards and is not for a single work |
| **BAFTA / Academy Award** | the respective academy's membership and juries | recognition within that academy's system | does not make the film recognised by the other academy or by any festival |

The last column is where the answer's precision lives. Every row's negative is a sentence a candidate might otherwise have written.

### 8. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** All this apparatus of distinctions serves a deflationary purpose. It tells a candidate how not to overstate an award, but it gives no account of why awards matter at all - and awards plainly do matter, for soft power, for the visibility of small films and for the international standing of a national cinema.

**Reply.** The objection is right that a purely deflationary treatment would be useless, and the positive account is available. Awards matter for three specifiable reasons. They **redistribute attention**, which is the one resource small and non-commercial films cannot buy - a prize in a named category is, in effect, a subsidy in publicity. They **create a record**: an award is dated, categorised and attributable, which is why the awards of a national system constitute a usable historical series in a way that box-office talk does not. And they **circulate a cinema beyond its own market**, which is the sense in which a national cinema acquires international standing. What the discipline adds is the limit: recognition by **one institution, in one category, by one jury, in one year** is not a measure of cultural value, and an answer that treats a prize as proof of worth has substituted a jury's judgement for an argument. The defensible formulation is therefore the soft-power thesis with its caveat: **international recognition expands visibility and is a poor measure of cultural value; the stronger claim for Indian cinema is the breadth of its domestic linguistic production, not the number of its foreign prizes.**

### Examination use

Any question on awards, festivals, cinema and soft power, or the international standing of Indian cinema is answered from this lesson. The mark-bearing structure is **the ladder, then the three qualifiers, then the soft-power thesis with its caveat**. The **award-year against ceremony-year** demonstration in section 4 is the single most distinctive piece of material here, because it can be shown with two consecutive editions and almost no candidate will have it. In an objective item, the recurring targets are the **1969** institution year, the **Swarna Kamal** and **ten lakh rupees** composition, **Devika Rani** as first recipient, the **industry committee** as selector, the **certification eligibility gate**, and the separateness of **BAFTA** from the **Academy Awards**.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing "won an international award" without the institution, the category and the year.
- Treating BAFTA and the Academy Awards as one institution, or either as a festival prize.
- Converting a festival **selection** into a nomination or a win.
- Equating **award year** with **ceremony year**.
- Naming a Dadasaheb Phalke or National Film Award recipient from memory, without a date.
- Describing the Dadasaheb Phalke Award as a category of the National Film Awards, or as an award for a particular film.
- Expanding a "first Indian film" claim beyond the exact category and dated source that supports it.
- Attributing the administration of the national awards to the Directorate of Film Festivals in the present tense.

### Revision notes

1. **The ladder:** festival **selection** -> **nomination** in a category -> **award** in a named category, year and institution. Each arrow is separately earned.
2. **National Film Awards** - national recognition across **feature**, **non-feature** and **cinema-writing** categories, conferred by the **President of India** under the **Ministry of Information and Broadcasting's** framework.
3. **The eligibility gate:** films **certified by the CBFC** within the relevant calendar year. A regulatory act is the precondition for consideration for a recognition - sequence, not identity.
4. **Dadasaheb Phalke Award** - **instituted 1969**; **India's highest award in cinema**; for **outstanding contribution to the growth and development of Indian cinema**; **selected by a Committee of eminent personalities from the Indian film industry**; comprises a **Swarna Kamal (Golden Lotus) medallion** and **ten lakh rupees**; commemorates **Dadasaheb Phalke (1870-1944)**, maker of ***Raja Harishchandra* (1913)**; first recipient **Devika Rani, 1969**.
5. The printed awarding body, the **Directorate of Film Festivals** (**1973**), was merged into **NFDC** by the **23 December 2020** decision. Use the two-clause form.
6. **71st edition:** Dadasaheb Phalke Award for **award year 2023** to **Mohanlal**; ceremony **23 September 2025**.
7. **72nd edition:** announced **18 July 2026** by the **National Film Award Cell, MIB**, for **award year 2024**, eligibility running to films **certified by the CBFC between 1 January 2024 and 31 December 2024**; conferred by **President Droupadi Murmu** on **22 September 2026** at **Ekta Nagar, Kevadia, Gujarat**; Dadasaheb Phalke Award to **Anant Nag**. Checked **29 September 2026**.
8. **The gap between award year and ceremony is about two years in both editions** - never collapse them.
9. **IFFI** - established **1952**; **Asia's oldest and pre-eminent** international film festival; a **competitive** festival recognised by the international federation of film producers' associations; organised by **NFDC** with the **Government of Goa** and the **Entertainment Society of Goa**.
10. **BAFTA** = awards of the **British Academy of Film and Television Arts**; **Academy Awards** = awards of the **US Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences**; **Cannes, Venice, Berlin** = prizes within distinct **festivals**. Three kinds of body; never interchangeable.
11. ***Boong*** - the official **2026** BAFTA result lists it as the winner in the **Children's and Family Film** category; **directed by Lakshmipriya Devi**. Keep the exact category, the exact credit, and tie any "first" claim to that category and a dated source.
12. Analytical reading, marked as such: such recognition can illustrate the visibility of **regional and Northeast Indian storytelling**, and global circulation can **widen the cultural map** through which Indian cinema is perceived.
13. **The soft-power verdict:** international recognition **expands visibility** and is a **poor measure of cultural value**; the stronger claim for Indian cinema is the **breadth of its domestic linguistic production**.
14. Three reasons awards nevertheless matter: they **redistribute attention**, they **create a dated record**, and they **circulate a cinema beyond its market**.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"International awards expand the visibility of Indian cinema but are a poor measure of its cultural value." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is correct in both halves, for different reasons.

**Why recognition expands visibility.** An award redistributes attention, the one resource small films cannot buy. It creates a dated, categorised, attributable record, and circulates a film beyond its language market. The **2026** BAFTA result listing ***Boong*** as winner in the **Children's and Family Film** category, **directed by Lakshmipriya Devi**, illustrates this: such recognition can carry regional and Northeast Indian storytelling into circulation it would not otherwise reach, widening the cultural map through which Indian cinema is perceived.

**Why it is a poor measure.** An award records **one institution**, **one category**, **one jury**, **one year**. Those four qualifiers are the whole of it. The systems are separate: the awards of the **British Academy of Film and Television Arts** are not those of the **US Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences**, nor a Cannes, Venice or Berlin festival prize; a festival **selection** is not even a nomination.

**The domestic comparison sharpens this.** India's system recognises **feature, non-feature and cinema-writing** work, and its highest honour - the **Dadasaheb Phalke Award**, **instituted 1969**, a **Swarna Kamal** and **ten lakh rupees**, decided by a **committee of eminent film-industry personalities** - rewards contribution to the **growth and development of Indian cinema**, not a single work.

**The concession:** visibility is not nothing; attention converts into finance, distribution and the survival of non-commercial practice.

**Verdict:** recognition is an instrument of circulation, not a criterion of worth; the stronger claim remains the breadth of India's domestic linguistic production.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the three specific mechanisms by which awards expand visibility 4 + the four qualifiers that bound what an award establishes 4 + the three international systems correctly distinguished as different kinds of body 4 + one dated, correctly categorised instance with its exact credit 3 + the domestic system used as a comparison, with the Phalke award's criterion and composition 3 + a conceded qualification and a verdict that is not merely dismissive 2.** Writing "won a BAFTA" without the category, or treating BAFTA and the Academy Awards as one body, forfeits three marks each, since the question turns on exactly those distinctions.

### Concept check

**Question:** A profile states: "He received the 2026 Dadasaheb Phalke Award, India's highest film honour, given by the Directorate of Film Festivals for his best film of the year." Identify every error and give the accurate version.

**Model answer:** There are **four errors**. **First, "the 2026 Dadasaheb Phalke Award"** collapses award year into ceremony year. The award conferred at the ceremony of **22 September 2026**, held at **Ekta Nagar, Kevadia, Gujarat**, was the Dadasaheb Phalke Award for **award year 2024**, conferred on **Anant Nag**; the winners of that **72nd** edition had been announced on **18 July 2026**. The preceding edition makes the pattern plain: the Phalke award for **award year 2023** went to **Mohanlal**, conferred at a ceremony on **23 September 2025**. In both editions the gap between award year and ceremony is about two years. **Second, "given by the Directorate of Film Festivals"** is historically correct and currently wrong. The printed source does name that Directorate, set up in **1973** and also charged with organising the National Film Awards functions, as the awarding body - but it was one of four units merged with **NFDC** by the Government decision dated **23 December 2020**. The accurate present-tense statement is that the award is conferred by the **President of India** under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting's national film awards framework. **Third, "for his best film of the year"** misstates the criterion. The award is for **outstanding contribution to the growth and development of Indian cinema** - a career and field-level criterion - which is why it honours a person rather than a work and is not a category of the National Film Awards. **Fourth, the selector is omitted**, and it is a substantive fact: the recipient is chosen by a **Committee consisting of eminent personalities from the Indian film industry**. **The accurate version:** *He received the Dadasaheb Phalke Award for award year 2024, instituted in 1969 as India's highest award in cinema for outstanding contribution to the growth and development of Indian cinema, selected by a committee of eminent film-industry personalities and comprising a Swarna Kamal medallion and a cash prize of ten lakh rupees; it was conferred by the President of India at the 72nd National Film Awards ceremony on 22 September 2026.*

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the date on a photograph of a ceremony as the date of the award. In this system a film year, an announcement and a conferral are three separate events spread across as much as two years, and the award belongs to the first of them.

## Lesson 14 - Film as a fragile object: preservation, archives and restoration

Progress: 14/16 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: why film decays; what conservation, restoration and digitisation each do and do not do; the institutional custody of Indian film preservation before and after the consolidation; selection bias in what gets saved; and the boundary with the heritage owner

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the canonical owner's preservation material on the fragility of the medium and on the institutional custody of preservation within the consolidated architecture; the archive's founding record; and the enrichment owner's preservation dimension on which films and records are restored, catalogued and made accessible
CA search: "Not sought at this lesson - the session's single current linkage has already been claimed and placed in Lesson 13"
CA found: None; the one current record in this session is in Lesson 13 and is not duplicated here
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the decay ladder, and the intervention that answers each rung

```text
 WHAT GOES WRONG                      WHAT ANSWERS IT
 ==============                       ===============

 RUNG 1  CHEMICAL DECAY OF THE BASE   PREVENTIVE CONSERVATION
   nitrate stock: unstable and          controlled temperature and
   hazardous                            humidity; correct storage;
   acetate stock: shrinks, warps,        minimal handling
   becomes acidic over time             -> acts on the ENVIRONMENT,
                                           not on the object
                |                                    |
 RUNG 2  LOSS OF THE IMAGE            CONSERVATION OF THE CARRIER
   colour fading; emulsion damage;      cleaning, repair, stabilising,
   scratches; sprocket damage;          rehousing the physical reel
   splice failure                       -> keeps the ORIGINAL usable
                |                                    |
 RUNG 3  FORMAT OBSOLESCENCE          MIGRATION
   the format survives but nothing      transfer to a readable format
   can read it; projection and          -> and then the NEW format
   playback equipment disappears           begins its own obsolescence
                |                           clock
                |                                    |
 RUNG 4  THE CONTENT IS DAMAGED       RESTORATION
   missing footage; degraded sound;     picture and sound restored from
   incomplete elements                  the best surviving elements
                                        -> an INTERPRETIVE act: someone
                                           decides what "restored"
                                           means
                |                                    |
 RUNG 5  THE OBJECT SURVIVES BUT      CATALOGUING AND ACCESS
         CANNOT BE FOUND OR SEEN       description, indexing,
   uncatalogued, unindexed,            provision for viewing
   inaccessible                        -> without this, preservation
                                          has preserved nothing usable

 +-------------------------------------------------------------+
 | THE RUNG EVERYBODY SKIPS: RUNG 0 - SELECTION.                |
 | Before any of the five, somebody decides WHICH films are     |
 | worth the cost. Commercial value rather than cultural value  |
 | usually decides what is restored. Every archive is therefore |
 | also a record of what a period thought was worth keeping.    |
 +-------------------------------------------------------------+
```

The ladder gives five interventions, each answering a different failure. The box gives the sixth question, which comes before all of them and is the one an analytical answer must raise.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Film preservation looks like a technical subject and is usually answered with a sentence about digitisation. That sentence is almost always wrong in a specific way: it treats digitisation as *the* solution, when digitisation answers one rung of the ladder and creates a new problem at another.

The material that makes this answerable is partly here and partly in the heritage topic, which owns the general conservation vocabulary. This lesson borrows that vocabulary deliberately - conservation, preventive conservation, restoration, access - and applies it to a medium with a distinctive property: unlike a monument, a film is **reproducible**, so preserving the *work* and preserving the *object* are two different projects that can come apart.

### 2. Why film decays, stated precisely

Film is a **fragile medium**. The canonical statement of its vulnerabilities names **nitrate and acetate decay**, **colour fading** and **obsolete formats**.

Take them one at a time, because each implies a different response.

**Base instability.** Early film stock is chemically unstable and, in the case of nitrate, hazardous. Later acetate stock deteriorates in its own way over time. This is decay of the **carrier**, and the response is environmental - storage conditions, handling discipline - rather than intervention on the object.

**Colour fading.** Colour images are chemically less stable than black-and-white ones, so a colour film can survive physically while losing the very property that distinguished it. This is a reminder that "the film survives" and "the film survives as it was" are different claims.

**Obsolete formats.** This is the vulnerability specific to recorded media and it has no analogue in built heritage. A monument does not stop being readable because the technology to read it has vanished; a film can. Preserving the reel is useless if nothing can play it, which is why **migration** to readable formats is a permanent, recurring obligation rather than a one-time act.

### 3. What each intervention actually does

**Preventive conservation** acts on the **environment**: temperature, humidity, storage, handling. It is the cheapest intervention and the one that prevents the largest amount of loss, and it is invisible, which is why it is chronically under-resourced.

**Conservation of the carrier** acts on the **object**: cleaning, repairing, stabilising, rehousing. It keeps the original element usable.

**Migration** moves content to a readable format. It answers obsolescence, and it immediately starts a new obsolescence clock on the new format. Migration is therefore a cycle, not a destination.

**Restoration** reconstructs picture and sound from the best surviving elements. It is the most visible intervention and the most **interpretive**: someone must decide which version is being restored, what counts as the work, and how far to intervene. The heritage topic's general point applies exactly - restoration returns an object toward a known earlier state on the basis of evidence, and choosing which earlier state is a judgement.

**Cataloguing and access** make the preserved object findable and viewable. Without them, preservation has kept something nobody can use.

**And the point about digitisation, which is where most answers go wrong.** Digitisation is a form of **migration**, and it does two things well: it provides access, and it reduces handling of the original, which is itself a major agent of damage. It does **not** conserve the carrier - the original reel continues to decay in its can whether or not a digital copy exists - and the digital file has its own fragilities: format obsolescence, storage-medium failure, and dependence on continuously maintained systems. The accurate formulation is that **digitisation secures access and reduces handling; it does not replace physical conservation, and it transfers rather than removes the obsolescence problem.**

### 4. Who holds film preservation in India

The two-clause discipline from Lesson 12 governs this section entirely.

**The founding fact.** Film preservation in India was institutionally housed in the **National Film Archive of India**, **established in 1964** as a unit under the **Ministry of Information and Broadcasting**, at **Pune**.

**The current arrangement.** The archive was one of the **four film media units** - with the Films Division, the Directorate of Film Festivals and the Children's Film Society, India - merged with the **National Film Development Corporation** pursuant to the Government of India decision dated **23 December 2020**. Its functions therefore now sit within the consolidated NFDC architecture, whose stated remit includes facilitating development, production, promotion and the **preservation of the rich heritage of films across various Indian languages**.

**The mission-level effort.** A dedicated **national film heritage mission** exists under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting to preserve, conserve, restore and digitise India's film heritage, implemented through the archive function. **Its year of launch could not be settled from an official source in this pass** - the dates in circulation disagree - and **no outlay, target, film count or project name is asserted anywhere in this session**. The mission is therefore taught by **function only**, and the uncertainty is declared rather than resolved by choosing a convenient figure.

**What is not claimed about the archive, stated openly.** No holdings figure, reel count, title list, acquisition record, restoration project name, budget, staffing figure or facility description is asserted. These are precisely the numbers an unsupported answer reaches for, and none is established here.

### 5. Rung 0: selection, and what an archive is a record of

Before any intervention there is a decision about **which** films receive it, and the canonical observation is blunt: **commercial value rather than cultural value usually decides what is restored.**

Three consequences follow, and they are the analytical heart of this lesson.

**Restoration follows the market.** Films with a living rights-holder, a recognisable star, a remake prospect or a re-release market are the films for which somebody will pay to restore. Films without those - regional production without a corporate owner, documentary, children's film, experimental work, films in smaller language markets - are systematically less likely to be restored, however important they are.

**The gap is not random; it tracks the same inequality as distribution.** Lesson 6 established that reach is unequally distributed across India's language markets. Preservation inherits that inequality, because the resources that restore a film are the resources that distributed it. A national film heritage is therefore at risk of being, by default, a heritage of whatever was commercially strongest.

**Therefore an archive is a double record.** It records the films it holds, and it records what the period that assembled it thought worth keeping. That second record is itself historical evidence, and noticing it is what separates an analytical answer from a description of archiving.

**The public rationale falls out of this directly.** Preservation has no commercial constituency for exactly the films that most need it. That is the argument for a **public** archive with a statutory or institutional mandate and its own resources - and it is also the precise reason the consolidation of Lesson 12 raises a question rather than settling one: an archive's preservation logic operates on decades, while a development corporation's operates on projects, and when they share a budget the longer horizon is the one that can be deferred without anything visibly failing this year.

### 6. Comparative treatment: preserving a film against preserving a monument

| Axis | A monument | A film |
|---|---|---|
| The object | unique; the fabric **is** the heritage | reproducible; many prints may exist and the "work" is not identical with any one of them |
| Principal threats | weathering, vegetation, salts, vibration, encroachment, disaster | chemical base decay, colour fading, physical damage, **format obsolescence** |
| Can the threat be prohibited? | partly - construction near it can be regulated | no - decay is continuous and internal to the material |
| Effect of copying | a replica is not the monument | a copy can carry the work faithfully, which changes what "loss" means |
| The distinctive risk | loss of authentic fabric through over-restoration | loss of **readability** - the object survives but nothing can play it |
| Where the real constraint lies | capacity: trained conservators, materials, monitoring | capacity **and** selection: who pays to restore what |

The decisive difference is in the first and fourth rows. Because film is reproducible, a faithful copy can preserve the work even if the original element perishes - which is why migration and digitisation are meaningful strategies for film in a way that replication is not for a monument. But this is also why the specific loss in film preservation is **readability** rather than authenticity, and why a preservation policy that funds copying but not storage has answered only half the problem.

### 7. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** Since film is reproducible and digital storage is cheap and getting cheaper, the preservation problem is essentially solved. Digitise everything and the question of which fragile reel decays in which can becomes an antiquarian concern rather than a policy one.

**Reply.** The objection contains a real truth - reproducibility genuinely does make film preservation different from monument conservation, and a faithful copy does carry the work - but its conclusion fails on three grounds. **First, digitisation is migration, not conservation.** It transfers the content to a format that is currently readable; it does nothing for the original carrier, and it starts a fresh obsolescence clock on the new format together with a new dependence on continuously maintained storage systems. A digital file is not a durable object; it is a commitment to ongoing institutional effort. **Second, "digitise everything" evades Rung 0.** Digitisation also costs money per title, so the selection question survives the technological change intact - and the canonical observation stands that commercial rather than cultural value usually decides what is treated. **Third, a digital copy is made from a source**, and the quality of what can be recovered depends on the condition of the element digitised; a badly decayed reel yields a badly degraded file, so storage conditions determine what digitisation can even capture. The defensible position is therefore that reproducibility changes the **shape** of the problem without dissolving it: preservation remains a continuing obligation combining environmental care of carriers, periodic migration, restoration where elements are damaged, cataloguing for access, and above all a selection policy that does not simply follow the market.

### Examination use

Any question on film preservation, film as heritage, archives, or cultural loss in the media is answered from this lesson. The winning structure is **the decay ladder, the matching interventions, the digitisation correction, the institutional custody in two clauses, and Rung 0**. The **selection point** - that commercial value usually decides what is restored, and that preservation therefore inherits the inequality of distribution - is the analytical move almost nobody makes and it is fully sourced. Note the boundary explicitly in an answer touching heritage: monument conservation, the UNESCO instruments and the general heritage machinery belong to the heritage owner; what is owned here is the conservation problem of a specific fragile and reproducible medium.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating digitisation as preservation rather than as migration plus access.
- Forgetting that the digitised file has its own obsolescence and storage dependency.
- Saying the archive "preserves India's films" in the present tense without the consolidation clause.
- Asserting a launch year, outlay, target or project name for the national film heritage mission.
- Asserting archive holdings, reel counts, budgets or restoration project names.
- Omitting Rung 0 and answering only the technical question.
- Treating film preservation as a sub-case of monument conservation, when reproducibility and format obsolescence make it structurally different.

### Revision notes

1. Film is a **fragile medium**: **nitrate and acetate decay**, **colour fading**, **obsolete formats**.
2. **Format obsolescence** is the vulnerability with no analogue in built heritage - the object can survive while becoming unreadable.
3. **Five interventions:** preventive conservation (environment) - conservation of the carrier (object) - migration (readability) - restoration (damaged content, and an **interpretive** act) - cataloguing and access (usability).
4. **Digitisation is migration plus access.** It reduces handling and secures access; it does **not** conserve the carrier and it **transfers** rather than removes the obsolescence problem.
5. A digital copy is only as good as the element it was made from, so storage conditions determine what digitisation can capture.
6. **Founding fact:** preservation was housed in the **National Film Archive of India**, **established 1964** under **MIB**, at **Pune**.
7. **Current arrangement:** the archive was one of the **four units** merged with **NFDC** by the decision dated **23 December 2020**; NFDC's stated remit includes preserving the film heritage across various Indian languages.
8. A **national film heritage mission** exists under MIB for preservation, conservation, restoration and digitisation; its **launch year, outlay and targets are not asserted here** because they could not be settled from an official source in this pass.
9. **No holdings, reel counts, budgets, staffing or project names** are asserted for the archive anywhere in this session.
10. **Rung 0 - selection.** **Commercial value rather than cultural value usually decides what is restored.**
11. Consequence: preservation **inherits the inequality of distribution**, so films without a commercial owner are least likely to be saved.
12. Therefore an archive is a **double record**: of the films it holds, and of what a period thought worth keeping.
13. **The public rationale:** the films that most need preservation have the least commercial constituency.
14. **Against a monument:** film is **reproducible**, so a faithful copy can carry the work - but the characteristic loss is **readability**, not authenticity.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"For cinema, preservation is a question of selection before it is a question of technology." Examine. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Correct, though the technology is real.

**The technical problem is definite.** Film suffers **nitrate and acetate decay**, **colour fading** and **format obsolescence** - the last distinctive: the object can survive unreadable. Five interventions answer it: **preventive conservation**, **carrier conservation**, **migration**, **restoration**, **cataloguing and access**.

**But each applies per title, so needs a prior decision.** **Commercial value rather than cultural value usually decides what is restored**: films with a rights-holder, a star or a re-release prospect attract expenditure; regional production without a corporate owner, documentary and experimental film do not.

**The consequence is structural.** Preservation inherits the inequality of distribution, so a heritage assembled by default is one of whatever was commercially strongest - the argument for public custody, housed in the **National Film Archive of India** from **1964**, now within the consolidated corporation.

**Digitisation does not dissolve it:** it is migration plus access, costs per title, and leaves selection intact.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the three named vulnerabilities, including format obsolescence 2 + at least four of the five interventions correctly distinguished 2 + the selection point stated as the sourced claim 2 + the inheritance-of-inequality consequence drawn 2 + the digitisation correction, that it is migration and access rather than conservation 1 + the institutional custody given in the two-clause form 1.** Writing that digitisation solves film preservation forfeits three marks, since it is the exact conflation the question is testing.

### Concept check

**Question:** A proposal states: "We will preserve India's film heritage by digitising every surviving print in the archive within five years, after which the physical reels can be deaccessioned." Assess the proposal using the decay ladder.

**Model answer:** The proposal answers two rungs, ignores three, and its final clause is the serious error. **What it answers.** Digitisation is a form of **migration**, so it addresses **format obsolescence** by moving content to a currently readable form; and by producing a viewing copy it addresses **access**, while simultaneously reducing **handling** of the original, itself a major agent of damage. Both gains are real. **What it ignores.** It does nothing for **preventive conservation** - the temperature, humidity and storage conditions in which the originals sit - and nothing for **conservation of the carrier**, so the physical elements continue to deteriorate through **nitrate and acetate decay** and **colour fading** during and after the project. It does not by itself perform **restoration** of damaged picture and sound, which is a separate and interpretive act requiring the best surviving elements. And it says nothing about **cataloguing**, without which a digitised collection is as unfindable as an undigitised one. **Why the final clause fails.** Deaccessioning the reels assumes the digital copy is a permanent substitute. It is not: the new format begins its own obsolescence clock, digital storage depends on continuously maintained systems, and a digital file made today may need migration again. More decisively, the quality of the copy is limited by the condition of the element it was made from, so if better conservation later permits a better transfer, the original is the only thing that makes that possible - and it will have been destroyed. **The omitted question is the first one.** Digitisation costs money per title, so a five-year universal programme will in practice be prioritised, and the canonical observation is that **commercial value rather than cultural value usually decides what is treated**. A defensible proposal would state its **selection policy** explicitly, fund **storage** alongside digitisation, budget for **recurrent migration**, and retain original elements rather than treat a copy as a terminal act of preservation.

**Misconception to avoid:** Thinking of preservation as a project with an end date. Every intervention on this ladder except restoration is **recurrent** - environments must be maintained, formats must be migrated again, catalogues must be kept current - so a preservation policy is a standing commitment, and any plan that promises completion in five years has misunderstood the obligation it is accepting.

```text
   BRIDGE       under the 2023 amendment, a film carrying an 'A' or
                'S' certificate needs a SEPARATE certificate to be
                exhibited on television or other prescribed media
                                |
  DESTINATION 3: AN ON-DEMAND ONLINE CATALOGUE
   INSTRUMENT   Information Technology (Intermediary Guidelines and
                Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021, notified
                25 February 2021 under the Information Technology
                Act, 2000
   WHO IS       "publishers of online curated content" - a curated
   COVERED      catalogue of audio-visual content, EXCLUDING news and
                current affairs, owned by, licensed by or contracted
                to be transmitted by the publisher, available ON DEMAND
   MECHANISM    a CODE OF ETHICS with SELF-CLASSIFICATION:
                  - classify content into age-appropriate categories
                  - restrict children's access to age-inappropriate
                    content, with an AGE VERIFICATION mechanism
                  - due discretion on content affecting the
                    sovereignty and integrity of India, national
                    security, or likely to disturb public order
                  - consider India's multiple races and religions
                    before featuring their beliefs and practices
                  - make content more accessible to disabled persons
   STRUCTURE    THREE TIERS:
                  1 self-regulation by the publisher
                  2 self-regulation by an association of publishers
                  3 oversight by the central government
   GRIEVANCE    complaint to the publisher, to be addressed in 15 DAYS
                -> escalation to the association, also 15 DAYS
                -> an INTER-DEPARTMENTAL COMMITTEE constituted by the
                   Ministry of Information and Broadcasting
   MINISTRY     publishes a charter for self-regulating bodies,
                issues advisories and orders, and holds EMERGENCY
                BLOCKING powers, subject to review by the
                inter-departmental committee; blocking directions are
                reviewed by a committee headed by the CABINET SECRETARY
   STATUS       the validity of the 2021 Rules has been challenged in
                various High Courts

 +---------------------------------------------------------------+
 | THE STRUCTURAL SHIFT, IN ONE LINE:                             |
 | PRIOR CERTIFICATION BY A STATUTORY BOARD   (cinema)            |
 |   -> A CONTENT CODE UNDER A BROADCASTING STATUTE  (television) |
 |     -> SELF-CLASSIFICATION UNDER A CODE OF ETHICS WITH         |
 |        THREE-TIER OVERSIGHT  (online curated content)          |
 | The obligation moves from a STATE BODY EXAMINING EACH WORK     |
 | to a PUBLISHER CLASSIFYING ITS OWN CATALOGUE, with the State   |
 | supervising the system rather than the individual film.        |
 +---------------------------------------------------------------+
```

That bottom box is the lesson in a sentence, and it is the analytical claim that any question about streaming and regulation is really asking for.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

The question "does the CBFC certify streaming content?" is asked constantly and answered with a bare no, which earns nothing. The useful answer explains **what replaced certification** and **how the replacement is structurally different**, and that requires holding three regimes side by side rather than one regime and two absences.

There is also a temptation to be resisted. Streaming regulation attracts strong opinions and a great deal of unreliable detail - platform names, individual controversies, litigation outcomes. This lesson stays on structure, records provenance, and declines the detail, which is both safer and, in an examination, more useful.

### 2. The first two regimes, restated so the third has something to contrast with

**Cinema.** Under the **Cinematograph Act, 1952**, a film requires **certification by the Central Board of Film Certification** before public exhibition. The Board **examines** each film and may certify it, **direct modifications and excisions**, or **refuse to sanction** it. The certificate is a **precondition** for exhibition, and exhibiting an uncertified film is a **cognisable and non-bailable** offence. This is **prior restraint** administered work by work, by a statutory body, with a categorical outcome.

**Television.** **CBFC certification is not required for television programmes and serials.** Programmes and advertisements on the cable television network are governed instead by the **Content/Advertisement Code** prescribed under the **Cable Television Network (Regulation) Act, 1995**, whose offences are **non-cognizable**. This is a **content code** - a standard applied to output - rather than an examination of each work before it is shown, and its enforcement is markedly lighter.

**The single exception inside the film regime**, worth restating because it is often confused with the television position: films made **especially for Doordarshan** do not require a CBFC certificate, because the official broadcaster has its own rules for examining such films. That is an exception within the film regime and is not the reason television content generally is outside it.

**And the bridge built in 2023.** Under the **Cinematograph (Amendment) Act, 2023**, a film carrying an **'A' or 'S' certificate requires a separate certificate** for exhibition on television or on any other media prescribed by the central government, and the Board may direct appropriate deletions or modifications for that separate certificate. So a **film** crossing into television is treated differently from television's own programming - a distinction well worth naming in an answer because it shows that the boundary is drawn around the **object** and its **route to the audience**, not simply around the screen.

### 3. The third regime, stated exactly

Online curated content is governed by the **Information Technology (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021**, notified on **25 February 2021** under the **Information Technology Act, 2000**, replacing the intermediary guidelines of 2011.

**Who is covered.** The Rules prescribe a framework for online publishers of **news and current affairs content** and, separately, of **curated audio-visual content**, the latter defined as a **curated catalogue of audio-visual content, excluding news and current affairs, owned by, licensed by, or contracted to be transmitted by the publisher, and available on demand**. Read the definition rather than the label: what is covered is a **catalogue**, made available **on demand**, and the exclusion of news and current affairs is definitional rather than incidental, because news publishers are dealt with under a different code within the same Rules.

**What the Code of Ethics requires of such publishers.** They must **classify content in specified age-appropriate categories**, **restrict access by children to age-inappropriate content** and **implement an age-verification mechanism**; **exercise due discretion in featuring content affecting the sovereignty and integrity of India, national security, or content likely to disturb public order**; **consider India's multiple races and religions before featuring their beliefs and practices**; and **make content more accessible to persons with disabilities**.

**How it is administered: three tiers.** The Rules institute a three-tier structure - **self-regulation by the publisher**, **self-regulation by an association of publishers**, and **oversight by the central government**.

**The grievance route.** A person aggrieved by a publisher's content may complain to the **publisher**, which must address the complaint within **fifteen days**. If dissatisfied, or if the complaint is not addressed in time, the complainant may escalate to the **association of publishers**, which must also respond within **fifteen days**. The complaint may then be considered by an **inter-departmental committee constituted by the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting**, where escalated under the specified conditions or referred by the Ministry itself.

**The Ministry's own powers.** It **publishes a charter for self-regulating bodies**, including Codes of Practices; it **issues advisories and orders** to publishers; and it holds powers to **block content on an emergency basis**, subject to review by the inter-departmental committee, with any blocking direction reviewed by a committee **headed by the Cabinet Secretary**.

**Status.** The **validity of the 2021 Rules has been challenged in various High Courts**. This session records that fact and asserts **no outcome**, no case name, no court and no finding.

**Provenance, stated rather than implied.** The account in this section is taken from a bounded check of a legislative-research summary of the Rules, checked **29 September 2026**. **No rule number is asserted**; **no platform, service or company is named**; **no enforcement instance, advisory, blocking order or litigation outcome is claimed**; and no assertion is made about how the framework operates in practice.

### 4. The comparison that answers the question

| Axis | **Cinema** | **Television** | **Online curated content** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Instrument | Cinematograph Act, **1952** | Cable Television Network (Regulation) Act, **1995** | IT (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, **2021** |
| Parent authority | a statutory Board under MIB | the content code framework | a Code of Ethics under the IT Act, supervised by MIB |
| Timing of control | **before** exhibition, work by work | **standards applied to output** | **self-classification by the publisher**, with complaint-driven review afterwards |
| Who classifies | the **Board** | - | the **publisher itself** |
| Age treatment | statutory categories: U, A, S, and UA 7+/13+/16+ | - | age-appropriate categories plus an **age-verification** mechanism |
| Characteristic remedy | modification, excision, refusal to sanction | code compliance; **non-cognizable** offences | complaint, escalation, ministerial advisory or order, emergency blocking |
| Breach at the exhibition end | **cognisable and non-bailable** | **non-cognizable** | not a certification offence; the Rules operate through the intermediary and publisher framework |

Read the "timing of control" and "who classifies" rows together and the structural shift is visible without any editorialising: control has moved **earlier in principle and later in practice** - the classification happens before publication, but it is performed by the publisher, and the State's involvement is triggered afterwards, by complaint or by its own reference.

### 5. What follows analytically, and what the regimes do not reach

**Three consequences that are fully supported.**

**The unit of regulation changes from the work to the system.** A Board examines a film; a three-tier framework supervises a **publisher and an industry association**. That is a different regulatory object, and it is the reason the streaming framework cannot simply be described as "certification for OTT".

**Age classification survives across all three regimes, which is revealing.** Of the four grounds in the film statute, the **age-group** ground is the one that reappears - the online code requires age-appropriate classification and an age-verification mechanism. Lesson 10 distinguished audience-protection grounds from community-offence grounds; the durability of the first across media, while the mechanism for the second becomes a matter of **due discretion** exercised by the publisher, is a substantive observation about where a broad consensus exists and where it does not.

**The remedy changes character.** In the film regime the characteristic remedy is applied **to the work** before release - cuts, or refusal. In the online regime the characteristic remedies are **procedural** - a complaint answered within fifteen days, an escalation, an advisory, an order - with emergency blocking as the exceptional instrument subject to a review layer. Procedural remedies are slower and more contestable than prior excision, which is simultaneously their virtue and their limitation.

**And the questions no regime here reaches.** The enrichment material identifies the tension precisely: **streaming access against platform concentration and discoverability**. Content rules govern what may be shown and how it must be classified; they say nothing about **which** films a catalogue acquires, how a recommendation system makes some works findable and others invisible, or how commissioning power is distributed. Those are questions of market structure and of the political economy of distribution, and they belong to the economy owner rather than to this session - a boundary worth stating explicitly in an answer, because naming a question you are not answering is stronger than ignoring it.

### 6. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** The three-regime account treats a difference of instrument as a difference of principle. In substance all three do the same thing - the State decides what audiences may see - and the online framework is arguably more intrusive than certification, since it operates through the threat of ministerial advisories and emergency blocking rather than through a published categorical decision that can be appealed.

**Reply.** The objection is serious and part of it must be granted. All three regimes do regulate what reaches audiences, and the online framework's ministerial advisory, order and emergency-blocking powers are genuine instruments of control, not merely supervisory formalities. But three differences of principle survive the collapse. **First, who decides in the first instance.** Certification is a decision by a statutory Board applying statutory grounds; online classification is a decision by the **publisher**, with the State's role beginning at a later tier. That is not a cosmetic difference: it determines who bears the primary judgement and who carries the cost of getting it wrong. **Second, the form of the decision.** A certificate is a categorical, published outcome with an appeal route - now to the **High Court** under section 5C. A procedural framework produces case-by-case outcomes through complaints and advisories, which is less transparent as a body of decisions even when each outcome is milder. **Third, what is being supervised.** One regime supervises **works**, the other supervises a **system of self-regulation** - and a regulator of systems has less control over any single work and more influence over an industry's standing practices. The defensible position is therefore that the three regimes differ genuinely in **who classifies, in what form, and at what stage**, while sharing a common object; and that the honest assessment notes both the shift away from prior examination and the new discretionary instruments that accompany it, without pretending that one is simply freer than the other.

### Examination use

Any question on regulating online content, on whether streaming should be certified, or on the reach of the certification regime is answered from this lesson. The mark-bearing structure is the **three-regime comparison**, followed by the **structural shift** in one sentence, followed by the **age-classification continuity** observation and the **market-structure boundary**. In an objective item, the discriminations that recur are: **no CBFC certificate for television programmes and serials**; the **cable statute's non-cognizable** offences against the film statute's **cognisable and non-bailable** one; the **2021 Rules** as the instrument for online curated content, with **self-classification** and a **three-tier** structure; and the **2023 amendment's separate certificate** for 'A' and 'S' films crossing to television or other prescribed media.

### Traps introduced here

- Saying the CBFC certifies streaming content, or that streaming is simply unregulated.
- Confusing the Doordarshan exception inside the film regime with the general position of television content.
- Treating the online framework as certification; it is **self-classification** under a code of ethics with tiered oversight.
- Omitting the **three tiers** or the **fifteen-day** grievance timelines.
- Asserting rule numbers, platform names, enforcement instances or litigation outcomes.
- Forgetting the **2023 bridge** - a separate certificate for an 'A' or 'S' film on television or other prescribed media.
- Answering a question about discoverability or platform concentration with content rules, which do not reach it.

### Revision notes

1. **Three regimes, three instruments:** the **Cinematograph Act, 1952** for cinema; the **Cable Television Network (Regulation) Act, 1995** for cable television content; the **IT (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021**, notified **25 February 2021** under the **IT Act, 2000**, for online curated content.
2. **Cinema:** prior **certification** by a statutory Board, work by work; certify, **modify and excise**, or **refuse to sanction**; breach is **cognisable and non-bailable**.
3. **Television:** **no CBFC certificate** for programmes and serials; a **Content/Advertisement Code**; offences **non-cognizable**.
4. **The 2023 bridge:** an **'A' or 'S'** film needs a **separate certificate** for television or other prescribed media, with possible deletions or modifications.
5. **Online curated content is defined** as a **curated catalogue** of audio-visual content, **excluding news and current affairs**, owned by, licensed by or contracted to be transmitted by the publisher, available **on demand**.
6. **The Code of Ethics requires:** age-appropriate **classification**; restriction of children's access with an **age-verification mechanism**; **due discretion** on sovereignty and integrity of India, national security and content likely to disturb public order; consideration of India's **multiple races and religions**; and **accessibility for disabled persons**.
7. **Three tiers:** publisher self-regulation -> association self-regulation -> **central government oversight**.
8. **Grievance:** publisher within **fifteen days** -> association within **fifteen days** -> an **inter-departmental committee** constituted by **MIB**.
9. **Ministry powers:** a **charter** for self-regulating bodies; **advisories and orders**; **emergency blocking**, reviewed by the inter-departmental committee, with blocking directions reviewed by a committee headed by the **Cabinet Secretary**.
10. The **validity of the 2021 Rules has been challenged in various High Courts**; no outcome is asserted here.
11. **The structural shift:** prior certification by a State body -> a content code -> **self-classification with tiered oversight**. The unit of regulation moves from the **work** to the **system**.
12. **Age classification survives across all three regimes**; the community-offence grounds become a matter of publisher **due discretion**.
13. **Remedies change character:** excision and refusal before release, against complaint, escalation, advisory, order and exceptional blocking afterwards.
14. **What no content regime reaches:** **platform concentration and discoverability** - which catalogues acquire what, and what a recommendation system makes visible. That belongs to the economy owner.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Compare the regulatory frameworks governing films in theatres, on television and on on-demand online platforms in India, and assess what the differences reveal about the direction of media regulation. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

Three destinations, three instruments, three legal characters.

**Cinema.** The **Cinematograph Act, 1952** requires **prior certification** by the **Central Board of Film Certification**, which may certify, **direct modifications and excisions**, or **refuse to sanction**. The certificate precedes exhibition; uncertified exhibition is **cognisable and non-bailable**. This is work-by-work prior restraint by a statutory body.

**Television.** **CBFC certification is not required** for programmes and serials. A **Content/Advertisement Code** applies under the **Cable Television Network (Regulation) Act, 1995**, whose offences are **non-cognizable**. The **2023** amendment builds a bridge: an **'A' or 'S'** film needs a **separate certificate** for television or other prescribed media.

**Online curated content.** The **IT (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021**, notified **25 February 2021**, cover a **curated on-demand catalogue** excluding news. Their **Code of Ethics** requires **self-classification** into age-appropriate categories, **age verification**, due discretion on sovereignty, national security and public order, regard for India's races and religions, and accessibility for the disabled. Administration is **three-tier** - publisher, association, central government - with **fifteen-day** grievance timelines and an **inter-departmental committee** under the Ministry, which issues advisories and orders and may block content in emergencies, subject to review by a committee headed by the **Cabinet Secretary**. The Rules' validity is under challenge in High Courts.

**What this reveals.** Regulation shifts from the **work** to the **system**, the classifier from a **statutory Board** to the **publisher**. Age classification persists across all three; community-offence grounds become publisher discretion.

**The limit:** none reaches **platform concentration or discoverability**, questions of market structure.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = three instruments named with correct years 3 + the timing-and-classifier contrast stated explicitly 3 + the three-tier structure with grievance timelines and the inter-departmental committee 3 + the 2023 separate-certificate bridge 2 + the age-classification continuity observation 2 + the discoverability limitation named as outside these regimes 2.** Saying that streaming is uncertified and therefore unregulated forfeits four marks, since it misses the entire 2021 framework the question is asking about.

### Concept check

**Question:** A film certified 'A' is released in theatres, later broadcast on television, and later still added to an on-demand online catalogue. State the applicable instrument and the operative requirement at each stage.

**Model answer:** **Stage one - theatrical release.** The **Cinematograph Act, 1952** applies. The film required **certification by the Central Board of Film Certification** before public exhibition; it holds an **'A'** certificate, restricted to an adult audience. The Board had three options - certify, **direct modifications and excisions**, or **refuse to sanction** - and the certificate is the **legal precondition** for exhibition, exhibiting an uncertified film being a **cognisable and non-bailable** offence. **Stage two - television broadcast.** Two things apply simultaneously and are often confused. First, **CBFC certification is not required for television programmes and serials**, which are governed by the **Content/Advertisement Code** under the **Cable Television Network (Regulation) Act, 1995**, whose offences are **non-cognizable**. But second, and decisively for this film, under the **Cinematograph (Amendment) Act, 2023** a film carrying an **'A' or 'S'** certificate requires a **separate certificate** for exhibition on television or on any other media prescribed by the central government, and the Board may direct appropriate **deletions or modifications** for that separate certificate. So the theatrical certificate does not carry the film onto television. **Stage three - the online catalogue.** No CBFC certificate is in issue. The applicable instrument is the **Information Technology (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021**, and the publisher of the curated on-demand catalogue must comply with the **Code of Ethics**: **classify** the content in age-appropriate categories, **restrict children's access** and operate an **age-verification mechanism**, exercise **due discretion** in relation to the sovereignty and integrity of India, national security and content likely to disturb public order, **consider India's multiple races and religions** before featuring their beliefs and practices, and make content **accessible to persons with disabilities**. Administration is **three-tier** - the publisher, an association of publishers and central government oversight - with **fifteen-day** timelines at the first two tiers and an **inter-departmental committee** constituted by the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting beyond them. **The pattern across the three stages** is that the classifier changes from a statutory Board to the publisher, and the State's role moves from examining the work to supervising the system.

**Misconception to avoid:** Thinking that a certificate travels with a film across media. It does not. The theatrical certificate does not authorise a television exhibition of an 'A' or 'S' film, and it has no role at all in an online catalogue, where the obligation is the publisher's own classification under a different instrument entirely.

## Lesson 16 - Building a cinema answer, and the ways it fails
