# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## The six slots, and the status vocabulary

- Every cinema claim occupies six slots: **film** (title, language, year), **person and role** (produced, directed, acted, sang playback, shot, exhibited), **date** (one of seven clocks), **instrument or institution**, **award and category**, **status**.
- The seven clocks: **shooting, exhibition, production, certification, award year, ceremony year, institutional founding or merger**.
- **Certification** is a legal precondition for public exhibition; **censorship** is modification or suppression; **selection** is programming; **nomination** is shortlisting in a category; **award** is a win in a named category, year and institution.
- **Award year** is not **ceremony year**. **Playback singing** is a credit distinct from acting and music direction. **Studio system** means standing company production. **Consolidation** is administrative merger.
- Three acts, never one: **certification** changes permissions; **recognition** changes reputation; **preservation** changes what survives.

## Cinema as composite form

- Cinema combines **narrative, performance, image and design, music and sound, editing** - five inputs each of which is an older Indian art practice.
- What it adds is not a sixth art but two properties: **mechanical reproducibility** and **mass simultaneity**. These explain both its cultural reach and the fact that its public exhibition is licensed by statute.
- Four layers produce meaning: **mise-en-scene** (what is arranged within the frame); **cinematography** (how the frame is made); **editing**, including **montage** (meaning by juxtaposition); **sound**.
- **Documentary** is a non-fiction form that **still involves selection and framing**; it is not an unmediated record. **Animation** is created through drawn, modelled or computer-generated frames.
- Consequence: a film is a **constructed account** at four levels - **direct** evidence of representation, **indirect** evidence of its subject, and an **incidental material record**. Film form is **choices under constraint**.
- Against theatre: cinema takes from the spectator the **choice of where to look**. Against photography: cinema adds the two **relational** layers, editing and sound.

## Arrival and indigenisation, 1896-1913

- **1896** Lumiere Brothers, **six silent films**, Bombay - first **exhibition**. **1897** *Coconut Fair and Our Indian Empire* - first film **shot** in India.
- **1899** **Harishchandra Bhatavdekar**, "**Save Dada**" - first venture **by an Indian**, **two shorts**, exhibited by him.
- **1900** **Major Warwick**, first cinema house, **Madras**. The theatre chapter separately names the **Gaiety Theatre**, by **Raghupathy Venkaiah**, as the first permanent cinema theatre there, without a year; both recorded, neither resolved.
- **1903** **Hiralal Sen**, *Indian Life and Scenes*; **F.B. Thanawalla**, *Taboot Procession* and *Splendid New Views of Bombay*. Early Indian film-making was **actuality** before narrative.
- **1907** **Jamshedji Madan**, **Elphinstone Picture House**, Calcutta. **1912** ***Pundalik***, **N.G. Chitre and R.G. Torney** - first **Indo-British collaboration**.
- **1913** ***Raja Harishchandra***, **Dadasaheb Phalke** - first **indigenous Indian silent feature**; also *Mohini Bhasmasur*, *Satyavan Savitri*; **1917** ***Lanka Dahan***, first **box-office hit**. **1916** Universal Studios, first Hollywood-based **agency**.
- Two structural readings: **exhibition preceded production**, so the audience was built before the industry; and the first features were **mythologicals**, so indigenisation occurred in **story** before technique.

## The silent decade, the companies, and the colonial gaze

- **1910-1920** silent films **accompanied by live music** - **sarangi, tabla, harmonium, violin**. The Indian film experience was **audio-visual from the start** and the accompaniment was Indian.
- **1918** **Kohinoor Film Company** and Phalke's **Hindustan Cinema Films Company**. Companies enabled film-makers, not the reverse: **Baburao Painter, Suchet Singh, V. Shantaram**.
- **Entertainment Tax**: **Calcutta 1922**, **Bombay 1923**. The State's first substantive engagement was **fiscal**, five years before the content inquiry.
- **1926** **Fatma Begum**, first Indian woman to **produce and direct** her own film, ***Bulbul-e-Paristan***.
- **1927** **Indian Cinematograph Committee**, founded by the **British Raj**, to examine the **adequacy of censorship** and the **"immoral effect"** of cinematograph films. Cinema became a governmental problem because of **mass reach** independent of literacy.
- The 1952 statute therefore **inherits** a colonial apparatus and rationale. Order of the period: **audience -> industry -> revenue -> regulation**.
- **Parsi theatre**, **1850s-1920s**, treated music as essential; **from the 1930s** many Parsi producers moved into film-making. **IPTA**, **1943-1947**, associated with **Balraj Sahni, Prithviraj Kapoor, Bijon Bhattacharya, Ritwik Ghatak, Utpal Dutt**; now in **Chhattisgarh, Punjab, West Bengal**.

## Sound, colour, studio and stunt, 1931-1939

- **1931** ***Alam-Ara***, **Ardeshir Irani** - first **talking** film; **W.M. Khan** sang **"De de khuda ke naam par"** **live on set**, the first **recorded** song. **Playback** - pre-recorded and lip-synced - is **1935**, **Nitin Bose**, **New Theatres**; the printed "first playback singer" wording for Khan is **reported, not asserted**.
- **1933** ***Sairandhri***, **Prabhat** - first Indian **colour** film, **processed in Germany**. **1937** ***Kisan Kanya***, **Ardeshir Irani** - first **indigenously made** colour film. The four-year gap measures **processing capability**.
- **1935** ***Devdas***, **P.C. Barua** - first **studio system**: contracted personnel, house styles, the star system, a bias toward volume, and thus the economic root of formula.
- **1935** ***Hunterwali***, **J.B.H. Wadia and Homi Wadia** - first Indian **stunt** films; **Mary Evans**, of **Australian origin**, "**Fearless Nadia**"; *Toofan Mail*, *Punjab Mail*, *Flying Rani* clustered without separate years.
- **1937** ***Naujawan***, **J.B.H. Wadia** - first film **without any song**. **1939** ***Premsagar***, **K. Subrahmanyam** - first **south Indian** film, as the source states it and not extended.
- Read the decade as **seven capabilities**, not seven titles; across it the audience became a **language, star and genre** audience.

## Many language markets

- **Sound caused the segmentation**: a silent film swaps intertitles, a talking film **is** in a language. This explains the **timing** that diversity alone cannot.
- The source records India as having **one of the largest film industries in Asia**, producing the **largest number of films in the world**, in **Hindi, Telugu, Tamil, Bhojpuri and others** - an illustrative list with no figure attached, and **no number is asserted** anywhere here.
- **Administrative evidence:** CBFC head office **Mumbai** with **nine** regional offices - Mumbai, Kolkata, Chennai, Bengaluru, Thiruvananthapuram, Hyderabad, New Delhi, Cuttack, Guwahati - and a **regional facilitation office at Chandigarh**; the core source names **eight** beside Mumbai, dealing specifically with **regional films**.
- **Every dubbed version needs a fresh certificate**; **imported foreign films** require certification; the only stated exception is films made **especially for Doordarshan**.
- **Full FDI** in the film sector drew **20th Century Fox**, **Warner Bros.** and foreign directors; dated by the source only as **"in recent years"**, and no year is asserted.
- Verdict line: Indian cinema is **multilingual by structure and unequal in reach**; national film policy is always also a **federal and linguistic** question. No individual regional film industry is narrated here.

## Two tracks: entertainment and parallel

- Cinema is **broadly of two forms**: one serving primarily as **entertainment**, the other termed **alternative** or **parallel**, seeking to reflect **the realities of everyday life**. The distinction is by **purpose**, not by quality.
- **Parallel cinema emerged in the late 1940s** as an **alternative to mainstream commercial films**; themes **poverty, social injustice and human suffering**; it **drew inspiration from literature and theatre**; associated with **Satyajit Ray, Ritwik Ghatak, Guru Dutt and Shyam Benegal**. **No film title, year or stylistic claim is attached to any of the four.**
- Two incidental sourced facts about one of the four: he **designed the plaque** of the national literary academy's award, and the **1995 Kolkata institute bears his name**.
- **Why a commercial track:** studio overheads require continuous output, and genre, stars and song make an audience **predictable**. **Why an alternative track survived:** smaller budgets, free material from literature and theatre, later institutional finance and festivals.
- The tracks are **mutually enabling**; the parallel track's narrow reach is **structural**, not a verdict on quality.
- Kinds of Indian film evidenced here: **mythological, stunt, social-reform, parallel, entertainment, documentary, children's, animation** - and no others.

## Representation, and the limits of film as evidence

- Periodisation of women's screen image: **silent** - restrictions on a woman's life; **1920-40** - emancipation themes (**child marriage ban**, **sati abolition**, then **widow remarriage, women's education, workplace equality**) under **V. Shantaram, Dhiren Ganguli, Baburao Painter**; **1960-80** - **extremely stereotypical**, glorifying **motherhood, fidelity, absurd sacrifice**; **parallel cinema** - women's **real struggles and liberation**; **current age** - the modern woman balancing **career, motherhood, identity**.
- It is the source's **reading of representation**, not social history, and it **leaves 1940-60 uncharacterised**.
- **The decisive inference:** representation **regressed** while formal rights advanced, so films are **not** a measurement of social conditions.
- **Counter-case:** **1935** stunt films with **Mary Evans**, "Fearless Nadia", as the female action lead - commercial motive, not reformist intent, produced that agency.
- **National identity:** post-Independence films **shaped our identity as a nation**, reached **both urban and rural** areas, **reflect socio-economic and political life**, **impact audiences deeply** and **create empathy with characters**. Cinema is the first Indian cultural form whose reach did not depend on **literacy**. The identity so formed is **weighted** toward dominant distribution centres.

## Music, song and the playback system

- Music was **in the hall** before it was **on the print**; 1931 relocated it rather than introducing it.
- **1931** produced recorded sound and the song as a **separately circulable recording**; its songs were sung **live on set**. **Playback** - pre-recording plus lip-sync, separating **voice** from **body** - arrives in **1935** with **Nitin Bose** at **New Theatres** (*Bhagya Chakra*; Hindi remake *Dhoop Chhaon*; music **Raichand Boral**, sound **Mukul Bose**), and the priority between the two versions is not adjudicated here.
- **1937** *Naujawan* as the first film **without any song** proves song had become the **default** within six years.
- Five-step mechanism: hall music (1910s) -> recorded sound with live on-set singing (1931) -> song as recording (1931) -> playback and the separate singing credit (1935) -> songlessness as a first (1937).
- **Formally**, a song sequence is mise-en-scene-intensive, mobile in camera, **cut to pre-existing music**, and - from **1935** - built on a pre-recorded track, so **image follows sound**, inverting the usual order. Functionally it suspends narrative time, relocates, and addresses emotion directly.
- **Boundary:** musical systems, forms, composers and later singers belong to the music owner and are not named here.

## Certification: statute, Board, powers, scope

- The **Cinematograph Act, 1952** was instituted **to certify films**; its major function was to flesh out the **constitution and functioning of the CBFC**.
- The Board was **set up in 1950** as the **Central Board of Film Censors**, **renamed** under the 1952 Act, is **directly under MIB**, head office **Mumbai**.
- **Composition:** a **Chairman** plus **not less than 12 and not more than 25** members, appointed by the **Central Government** through MIB, for a term of **three years or more**; members usually film-industry personalities or other intellectuals.
- **Four grounds** for deciding a film should not be exhibited: offence to a **geographical area**, **age group**, **religious denomination**, **political group** - three community-offence grounds and one audience-protection ground.
- **Three outcomes:** certify; **direct modifications and excisions**; **refuse to sanction**. The middle one is **conditional permission** and is where the censoring effect lives.
- **Federal split:** certification is **Union**; **enforcement of censorship** lies with the **States**.
- **Scope:** all films for public exhibition; **imports**; every **dubbed** version needs a **fresh** certificate. **Only stated exception:** films made **especially for Doordarshan**. **TV programmes and serials** are outside, under the **Content/Advertisement Code** of the **Cable Television Network (Regulation) Act, 1995**.
- **Offence asymmetry:** uncertified exhibition is **cognisable and non-bailable**; cable-statute offences are **non-cognizable**.
- **Verdict line:** certification **in form**, partly censorship **in effect**; the distinction holds legally more firmly than practically. Constitutional doctrine belongs to the polity owner.

## The ladder of film law, and its dead statements

- **1927** Committee -> **1950** Board of Film Censors -> **1952** Act (renamed; **U**, **A**) -> **1983** Cinematograph (Certification) Rules upgradation adding **UA** (parental guidance, children under 12) and **S** (specialised audience) -> **1995** cable statute -> **2021** omission of section **5D** -> **2023** amendment.
- **1983** solved **two different axes**: UA the **age** axis, S the **occupation** axis - and it was done through **rules**, not a statutory amendment.
- **2021:** the **Tribunals Reforms Act** omitted section **5D**, which had constituted the **Film Certification Appellate Tribunal**, with retrospective effect from **4 April 2021**. The current appeal route is section **5C**, to the **High Court** - a general tribunals-reform measure, not a film-policy decision.
- **2023:** the **Cinematograph (Amendment) Act** replaces **UA** with **UA 7+**, **UA 13+**, **UA 16+**, **retains U, A and S**, and makes the age endorsement **advisory to parents or guardians** and not enforceable by others. It requires a **separate certificate** for an **'A' or 'S'** film on television or other prescribed media; creates offences of **unauthorised recording** and **unauthorised exhibition**, including attempt, with **Copyright Act, 1957** exemptions for private or personal use, current-affairs reporting and review or critique, punishable by **three months to three years** and a fine of **three lakh rupees to five per cent of audited gross production cost**; makes certificates **perpetually valid**; and **removes the central government's revisional power**.
- **Four dead statements:** the appellate tribunal as current; UA as parental guidance under 12; ten-year certificate validity; ministerial revision of certification.
- The amendment runs in **two directions**: narrowing discretionary control while extending reach and creating offences.

## Institutions by function, and the consolidation

- **Six jobs:** certify, train, archive, develop and produce, make documentary and children's film, exhibit and reward - with different horizons, constituencies and conflict risks.
- **CBFC** 1950/1952. **FTII, Pune** set up **1960** as the **Film Institute of India**, **Television Wing 1971**, re-designated, **registered as a Society in October 1974** under the **Societies Registration Act, 1860**; Governing Council under a Chairman, Academic Council, Standing Finance Committee; Film and Television wings. **SRFTI, Kolkata** established **1995**, autonomous under MIB, registered under the **West Bengal Societies Registration Act, 1961**, named after **Satyajit Ray**.
- **NFAI, Pune** established **1964** for **film preservation**. **NFDC** set up **1975**, absorbing the **Film Finance Corporation** and the **Indian Motion Picture Export Corporation** in **1980**. **Films Division** and the **Children's Film Society, India** are the documentary and children's bodies, founding years commonly given as **1948** and **1955** and recorded with a corroboration caveat.
- **Directorate of Film Festivals** set up **1973** to organise national and international film festivals, and **also** charged with organising the **National Film Awards** functions. **IFFI** established **1952**, **Asia's oldest and pre-eminent** international film festival, a **competitive** festival recognised by the international federation of film producers' associations, organised by **NFDC** with the **Government of Goa** and the **Entertainment Society of Goa**.
- **Consolidation:** Government of India decision dated **23 December 2020** merging **four** units - **Films Division, Directorate of Film Festivals, National Film Archive of India, Children's Film Society, India** - with **NFDC**. The **CBFC, FTII and SRFTI were not merged**.
- Say **consolidated, not created**. Use the **two-clause form**: founding fact, then current arrangement. Report the **rationale** and the **horizon risk**; assert no outcome.

## Awards, festivals and the recognition ladder

- **Ladder:** selection (programmed) -> nomination (shortlisted in a category) -> award (won, in a named category, year and institution).
- **National Film Awards** - **feature**, **non-feature** and **cinema-writing** categories, conferred by the **President of India** under MIB's framework. **Eligibility gate:** films **certified by the CBFC** within the relevant calendar year.
- **Dadasaheb Phalke Award** - **instituted 1969**; **India's highest award in cinema**; for **outstanding contribution to the growth and development of Indian cinema**; **selected by a Committee of eminent personalities from the Indian film industry**; a **Swarna Kamal (Golden Lotus) medallion** and **ten lakh rupees**; commemorates **Dadasaheb Phalke (1870-1944)**, maker of ***Raja Harishchandra* (1913)**; first recipient **Devika Rani, 1969**.
- **Two dated editions:** award year **2023** to **Mohanlal**, conferred **23 September 2025** at the **71st** edition; award year **2024** to **Anant Nag**, announced **18 July 2026** and conferred by **President Droupadi Murmu** on **22 September 2026** at **Ekta Nagar, Kevadia, Gujarat**, at the **72nd** edition. **The gap is about two years in both cases.**
- **Three international systems, three kinds of body:** **BAFTA** (British Academy of Film and Television Arts), the **Academy Awards** (US Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences), and prizes **within** the **Cannes, Venice and Berlin** festivals.
- ***Boong*** - the official **2026** BAFTA result lists it as winner in the **Children's and Family Film** category; **directed by Lakshmipriya Devi**. Keep the exact category and credit; tie any "first" to that category and a dated source.
- **Why awards matter:** they **redistribute attention**, **create a dated record** and **circulate a cinema beyond its market**. **What bounds them:** one institution, one category, one jury, one year.
- **Soft-power verdict:** international recognition **expands visibility** and is a **poor measure of cultural value**; the stronger claim is the **breadth of India's domestic linguistic production**.

## Preservation, decay and selection

- **Vulnerabilities:** **nitrate and acetate decay**, **colour fading**, **obsolete formats** - the last with no analogue in built heritage, since the object can survive while becoming unreadable.
- **Five interventions:** **preventive conservation** (environment); **conservation of the carrier** (object); **migration** (readability, and a recurring cycle); **restoration** (damaged content, an **interpretive** act); **cataloguing and access** (usability).
- **Digitisation is migration plus access.** It secures access and reduces handling; it does **not** conserve the carrier and it **transfers** the obsolescence problem. A copy is only as good as the element it was made from.
- **Custody, two clauses:** preservation was housed in the **NFAI**, **established 1964** at **Pune**; its functions now sit within the consolidated **NFDC**, whose stated remit includes preserving the film heritage across various Indian languages. A **national film heritage mission** exists under MIB for preservation, conservation, restoration and digitisation; **its launch year, outlay and targets are not asserted here**.
- **Rung 0 - selection.** **Commercial value rather than cultural value usually decides what is restored**, so preservation **inherits the inequality of distribution**, and an archive is a **double record** - of what it holds and of what a period thought worth keeping. Hence the public rationale.
- **Against a monument:** film is **reproducible**, so a faithful copy can carry the work; the characteristic loss is **readability**, not authenticity.

## The digital boundary

- **Three regimes:** the **Cinematograph Act, 1952** (prior certification by a statutory Board); the **Cable Television Network (Regulation) Act, 1995** (a content code, **non-cognizable** offences, **no CBFC certificate** for TV programmes and serials); the **IT (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021**, notified **25 February 2021** under the **IT Act, 2000** (self-classification under a Code of Ethics).
- **Online curated content** is a **curated catalogue** of audio-visual content, **excluding news and current affairs**, owned by, licensed by or contracted to be transmitted by the publisher, available **on demand**.
- **Code of Ethics:** age-appropriate **classification**; restriction of children's access with an **age-verification mechanism**; **due discretion** on sovereignty and integrity of India, national security and content likely to disturb public order; consideration of India's **multiple races and religions**; **accessibility for disabled persons**.
- **Three tiers:** publisher -> association of publishers -> central government oversight. **Fifteen-day** timelines at the first two tiers; an **inter-departmental committee** constituted by **MIB**; the Ministry publishes a **charter**, issues **advisories and orders**, and holds **emergency blocking** powers reviewed by a committee headed by the **Cabinet Secretary**. The Rules' **validity has been challenged in various High Courts**; no outcome asserted.
- **2023 bridge:** an **'A' or 'S'** film needs a **separate certificate** for television or other prescribed media.
- **The structural shift:** prior certification by a State body -> a content code -> **self-classification with tiered oversight**; the unit of regulation moves from the **work** to the **system**, and **age classification survives across all three regimes**.
- **What no content regime reaches:** **platform concentration and discoverability**, which belong to the economy owner.

## Answer construction and fact control

- Read **directive and object separately**; route the object to its dimension; open with a **qualified thesis**, never a definition.
- **Six dimensions:** form, representation, political economy, regulation, preservation, soft power. Answer the named one and add **one** more.
- **Body pattern:** claim -> **named, dated** evidence -> analysis -> qualification. **Mark scaling** is paid for in dimensions and verdict precision, not extra examples.
- **Seven theses available:** indigenisation, certification, two-cinemas, representation, plurality, institutional, preservation.
- **Two prohibited narratives:** the triumph story, replaced by the structural claim about multilingual breadth with the poor-measure caveat; and the censorship lament, replaced by the scheme-and-powers analysis.
- **Never write:** an output, screen, revenue or ranking figure; a year for the foreign-investment provision; a filmography for any of the four parallel-cinema figures; an award recipient without a date; an archive holding, budget or project name; a launch year or outlay for the film heritage mission; a rule number, platform name or litigation outcome under the 2021 Rules; a section number for the piracy offences; an asserted effect of the consolidation.
- **Close with a verdict that answers the exact wording** and could not be transplanted into another answer.

## Bounded-draft and bounded-research declaration

This session was generated as a bounded draft under the bounded-research rule. The substantive base is the complete core owner, the complete enrichment owner and one core OCR art and culture source. External work was confined to three declared purposes.

**(a) PYQ verification and routing.** The routing ledgers and the locally held official papers named in the manifest above. The **provisional 2026 Set-A key was not consulted**, and no answer letter is recorded or inferred anywhere. The reproducing core source prints its own answer keys beneath the reproduced objective list in its awards chapter; those keys were **encountered during extraction and were not carried into this session**.

**(b) One bounded material-fact pass.** The list of items was fixed before searching and is reproduced here in full, with the outcome of each. The final row was added at the repair stage, after an independent audit identified a factual blocker; it was resolved by the same bounded method and is flagged as such in its outcome cell and in Discrepancy 19.

| Item | Why it was on the list | Outcome |
|---|---|---|
| The **founding facts, character and registration status of the two film-training institutes** | the user-stated scope requires FTII and SRFTI as owned institutions; the core OCR source carries **neither institute at all**, and the canonical owner carries FTII only at the level of a founding year and a society status, with SRFTI absent entirely. Omitting them would leave the training function of the institutional map unevidenced | **Resolved from official organisational records.** The Pune institute was set up in **1960** as the **Film Institute of India** under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting, acquired a **Television Wing in 1971** and was re-designated, and was **registered as a Society in October 1974** under the **Societies Registration Act, 1860**, with a Governing Council under a Chairman, an Academic Council and a Standing Finance Committee, and Film and Television wings offering post-graduate diploma programmes. The Kolkata institute was **established in 1995** as an autonomous educational institution under the same Ministry, **registered under the West Bengal Societies Registration Act, 1961**, and is **named after Satyajit Ray**. **No intake, admission process, fee, campus history, alumnus, head, director or controversy is asserted for either** |
| The **founding year, objective and absorption history of the public film development corporation**, and the **exact scope and date of the consolidation** | the canonical owner records a 23 December 2020 decision and a consolidated architecture but not the corporation's own founding or prior absorptions; the user-stated scope requires the merged institutional arrangements to be taught with a currentness caution | **Resolved from official organisational records.** The corporation was **set up in 1975** with the objective of planning, promoting and organising an integrated and efficient development of the Indian film industry in accordance with national economic policy; in **1980** the **Film Finance Corporation** and the **Indian Motion Picture Export Corporation** were merged with it; and pursuant to the Government of India decision dated **23 December 2020**, the functions of the **Films Division**, the **Directorate of Film Festivals**, the **National Film Archive of India** and the **Children's Film Society, India** are merged into it. **No completion date for the integration, and no effect of the merger on any function, is asserted** |
| The **founding years of the public documentary body and the children's film body** | both are named in the user-stated scope and both appear in the consolidation, but neither the core OCR source nor the canonical owner supplies a founding year for either | **Partially resolved, and the limit declared.** The years commonly given are **1948** for the documentary body and **1955** for the children's film body. The official page that would have confirmed them could **not be opened directly in this pass**, so both years are carried **with a corroboration caveat** in the teaching, the register notes and Map 5. What **is** confirmed from official material is that both bodies existed and that their functions are merged into the development corporation |
| The **status, establishment and current organising arrangement of India's international film festival** | the canonical owner names the festival only as an international platform hosted in India; the user-stated scope requires festivals to be taught with the selection-against-prize distinction, which turns on whether the festival is competitive | **Resolved from the festival's own official record.** It was **established in 1952**, is **Asia's oldest and pre-eminent** international film festival, is recognised as a **Competitive Film Festival** by the **International Federation of Film Producers' Associations**, and is **organised by the development corporation** under the Ministry, in association with the **Government of Goa** and the **Entertainment Society of Goa**. **The year in which Goa became the festival's host, and any award name or category of the festival, were not established in this pass and are not asserted** |
| The **content of the current amending legislation on film certification** beyond the category markers and the appeal route already carried by the canonical owner | the user-stated scope requires the legal and institutional evolution of certification; the core OCR source's account ends at the 1983 rules and the since-omitted appellate tribunal, and the canonical owner carries only the markers, section 5C and the omission of section 5D | **Resolved at the level of scheme, with the provenance stated.** The amendment replaces the single intermediate category with **UA 7+, UA 13+ and UA 16+** while retaining **U, A and S**; makes the age endorsement advisory to **parents or guardians** and not enforceable by others; requires a **separate certificate** for an 'A' or 'S' film on television or other prescribed media; creates offences of **unauthorised recording and unauthorised exhibition**, including attempt, with **Copyright Act, 1957** exemptions and punishment of **three months to three years** and a fine of **three lakh rupees to five per cent of audited gross production cost**; makes certificates **perpetually valid**; and **removes the central government's revisional power**. The category markers and the appeal route rest on the canonical owner's record of the official consolidated text; the remaining items rest on a **legislative-research summary of the amending legislation, checked 29 September 2026**. **No section number is asserted for the piracy provisions, and no notification, rule-making or commencement date is claimed** |
| The **framework governing curated on-demand online content** | the user-stated scope expressly requires OTT and digital boundaries; neither the core OCR source nor the canonical owner carries any instrument for online content, so the boundary of the certification regime could not otherwise be drawn | **Resolved at the level of scheme, with the provenance stated.** Online curated content falls under the **Information Technology (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021**, notified **25 February 2021** under the Information Technology Act, 2000; the definition, the Code of Ethics requirements, the three-tier structure, the fifteen-day grievance timelines, the inter-departmental committee under the Ministry, the Ministry's charter, advisory, order and emergency-blocking powers, and the Cabinet Secretary-headed review of blocking directions are all taught as recorded. The source is a **legislative-research summary of the Rules, checked 29 September 2026**. **No rule number, platform, service, company, enforcement instance, advisory, blocking order or litigation outcome is asserted**, and the recorded fact that the Rules' validity has been challenged in various High Courts is carried **without any outcome** |
| A **launch year, outlay and scope for the national film heritage mission** | the user-stated scope requires film preservation and restoration to be owned, and the canonical owner names no mission | **Not resolved, and therefore not asserted.** A mission for the preservation, conservation, restoration and digitisation of India's film heritage under the Ministry is recorded **by function only**. The **launch year is given differently by different sources**, and the outlay and target figures in circulation could not be confirmed from an official page that opened in this pass. **No year, outlay, target, film count or project name is carried anywhere in this session** |
| Whether a **more recent national film awards edition** than the one recorded by the canonical owner had been conferred | the canonical owner's latest located record, rechecked 4 September 2026, names a recipient for award year 2023; if a later edition existed, every award statement in this session would be a photograph out of date at the moment of writing | **Resolved, and adopted as this session's single current linkage.** The **72nd** edition's winners were announced on **18 July 2026** by the National Film Award Cell of the Ministry, for the **2024** award year, with eligibility running to films certified by the Board between **1 January 2024 and 31 December 2024**; the awards were conferred by the **President of India** on **22 September 2026** at **Ekta Nagar, Kevadia, Gujarat**, together with the **Dadasaheb Phalke Award for award year 2024**, conferred on **Anant Nag**. The ceremony date and venue rest on the Ministry's own press-release record and the conferral on the President's Secretariat's press-release record; the **full text of the Secretariat's release could not be opened directly in this pass**, so the recipient's name, the announcement date, the awarding cell and the eligibility window are carried from that release together with contemporaneous reference material, with that status stated. **No category-by-category list of that edition's other winners is asserted** |

| Whether **playback**, as this session defines it, can be dated to **1931** and attached to **W.M. Khan** | an independent audit found that the session asserted the core source's "India's first playback singer" wording as plain fact while simultaneously defining playback as a **pre-recorded** song lip-synced on set - an internal contradiction, since *Alam-Ara*'s songs were captured live on set. The syllabus scope requires music and technology in cinema to be owned, so the point could not be left unresolved | **Resolved at the level of technique and year, with priority left open.** Playback in that sense is associated with **Nitin Bose** at **New Theatres**, Calcutta, in **1935**, in the Bengali ***Bhagya Chakra*** and its Hindi remake ***Dhoop Chhaon***, with **Raichand Boral** as music director and **Mukul Bose** as sound recordist; before it, film songs were recorded **live on set** as the performer sang. Established from a bounded check of reference material at the repair stage, checked **29 September 2026**, and recorded as **Discrepancy 19**. **No claim is asserted as to which of the two 1935 versions was completed first, no further singer, composer or recordist is named, and no biography, filmography or later career is given for any of the four names** |

**(c) One bounded current linkage.** Exactly one is claimed, file-wide, in **Lesson 13**: the **72nd National Film Awards of 22 September 2026** and the **Dadasaheb Phalke Award for award year 2024**. Every other pre-teach checklist in this session records the absence of a sought linkage truthfully. The **71st** edition's award for **award year 2023**, and the **2026** BAFTA result for *Boong*, are used as **dated records carried by the canonical owner**, not as additional current linkages.

## Discrepancies recorded rather than resolved

| # | Discrepancy | How this session handles it |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | The core OCR source's cinema chapter states that **Major Warwick** established the first cinema house in **Madras in 1900**, while its theatre chapter states that the **Gaiety Theatre**, set up by **Raghupathy Venkaiah**, was the **first permanent cinema theatre** in the then Madras, with no year attached | Lesson 3 prints both, notes that the qualifier *permanent* is the possible reconciling term, resolves neither, and uses neither as a decisive "first" in any model answer |
| 2 | The same person is spelled **Hiralal Sen** in the cinema chapter and **Hira Lala Sen** in the theatre chapter | Lesson 3 records both forms explicitly and adopts neither silently |
| 3 | The cinema chapter calls ***Raja Harishchandra*** the **first indigenous Indian silent film**, while the awards chapter calls it **India's first full-length feature film** | Lessons 3 and 13 print each chapter's wording in its own place, record the variance, and do not merge them into a third formulation |
| 4 | The cinema chapter states that ***Premsagar*** (**1939**) was the **first south Indian film**, which sits oddly beside the same source's record of a cinema house in Madras from **1900** | Lesson 5 reproduces the claim exactly as printed, records that it is not independently corroborated here, and expressly refuses to convert it into a first in any named language |
| 5 | The cinema chapter names **eight** CBFC regional offices beside the Mumbai head office; the canonical owner records **nine** regional offices plus a regional facilitation office at Chandigarh | Lessons 6 and 10 print both counts, record the variance, and rest the plurality argument on the existence of a distributed regional structure rather than on the number |
| 6 | The cinema chapter describes the **Film Certification Appellate Tribunal** under section **5D** as a major provision of the 1952 Act, while the canonical owner records that section **5D was omitted** by the Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021 with retrospective effect from **4 April 2021**, appeals now lying under section **5C** to the High Court | Lesson 11 prints the older provision as the position before 2021, states the current route, and records the change explicitly as a dead statement rather than silently correcting it |
| 7 | The cinema chapter describes **UA** as unrestricted exhibition subject to parental guidance for children **under 12**, added by the 1983 rules, while the canonical owner records the current **UA 7+, UA 13+ and UA 16+** markers | Lesson 11 gives the 1983 formulation on its rung of the ladder and the current markers on theirs, and records the older formulation among the four dead statements |
| 8 | The cultural-institutions chapter states that the **Directorate of Film Festivals**, set up **1973**, organises national and international film festivals and holds the additional charge of the National Film Awards functions, and the awards chapter names it as the awarding body of the highest cinema award; official organisational material records that Directorate as one of four units whose functions were merged into the development corporation by the **23 December 2020** decision | Lessons 12 and 13 use the two-clause form throughout - founding fact, then current arrangement - and record the printed attribution as correct history and inaccurate present tense |
| 9 | The cinema chapter states that the Indian film industry is **well known for producing the largest number of films in the world** and is **one of the largest in Asia**, without any figure, date or source | Lesson 6 uses both as the source's characterisations, asserts **no number, ranking or date**, and says so explicitly |
| 10 | The cinema chapter states that **in recent years** a provision was passed allowing full foreign direct investment in the film sector, with no year | Lesson 6 reproduces the statement as a characterisation and asserts no year, cap, policy name or investment figure |
| 11 | The theatre chapter gives the people's theatre association's abbreviation inconsistently within a single passage | Lesson 4 records the association by its full name, notes the inconsistency, and does not rely on either abbreviation |
| 12 | The cinema chapter's parallel-cinema section refers the reader to a diagram which, in the held copy, is an image carrying no recoverable text | Lesson 7 teaches the movement from the chapter's prose alone and declares that the diagram supplied no name, date or claim to this session |
| 13 | The cinema chapter carries sidebar headings for **South Indian Cinema**, **Famous Indian Cinema Controversies** and a question on a **National Film Policy**, with no recoverable body text in the held copy | Lessons 6 and 12 record the headings as evidence of the topics the source intended to cover, supply nothing under them, and enter each as a declared gap |
| 14 | The talkies table clusters ***Toofan Mail***, ***Punjab Mail*** and ***Flying Rani*** with the 1935 stunt entry without attaching separate years | Lesson 5 records the cluster as given and attaches no individual dates to those three titles |
| 15 | The awards chapter records that **the first Indian to bag** the highest cinema award was **Devika Rani** in **1969**, a phrasing that is odd for an award all of whose recipients are Indian | Lesson 13 records the fact and the year as printed and does not repeat the phrasing as though it drew a distinction |
| 16 | The commonly given founding years of the public documentary body and the children's film body could not be confirmed from an official page that opened in this pass | Lesson 12, the register notes and Map 5 carry both years with an explicit corroboration caveat, and the bounded material-fact pass table above declares exactly what was and was not established |
| 17 | The launch year of the national film heritage mission is given differently by different sources | Lesson 14 teaches the mission by **function only** and asserts no year, outlay or target anywhere in this session |
| 18 | The full text of the President's Secretariat release for the 72nd National Film Awards could not be opened directly in this pass, although its record of the conferral was located | Lesson 13 records the recipient with that stated status, and the ceremony date and venue rest separately on the Ministry's own press-release record |

| 19 | The core OCR source's talkies table describes **W.M. Khan** as **India's first playback singer** for *Alam-Ara* (**1931**), in the same entry that records his song as the **first recorded song**; **playback** in the sense used throughout this session - a song **pre-recorded** and then played back on set for the performer to **lip-sync** - is associated with **Nitin Bose** at **New Theatres**, Calcutta, in **1935**, in the Bengali ***Bhagya Chakra*** and its Hindi remake ***Dhoop Chhaon***, with **Raichand Boral** as music director and **Mukul Bose** as sound recordist | Lessons 5 and 9 print the source's wording as the source's, record that it is **not independently corroborated here** and is **inconsistent with this session's own definition of playback**, and teach the distinction explicitly: *Alam-Ara*'s songs were sung **live on set**, and playback proper is a **1935** technique. One safe exam sentence is fixed and repeated in the firsts matrix, the register notes, the maps and the fact-control block - **W.M. Khan sang "De de khuda ke naam par" in *Alam-Ara* (1931), recorded as the first song in an Indian film; the printed description of him as India's first playback singer is reported rather than relied on.** No claim is made about which of the two 1935 versions was completed first, and no further singer, composer or recordist is named |

## Declared gaps - material a candidate may expect that this session does not supply

1. **No film title, year, style, training, region, language of work, influence or international standing for Satyajit Ray, Ritwik Ghatak, Guru Dutt or Shyam Benegal.** They are named only as the figures the sources associate with the parallel tradition. The two incidental facts recorded about one of them - the literary academy's plaque and the naming of the 1995 Kolkata institute - are the only individual attributions made.
2. **No account of the development of any individual regional film industry** - Tamil, Telugu, Malayalam, Kannada, Bengali, Marathi, Assamese, Bhojpuri or any other. No regional studios, stars or production figures.
3. **No history of Indian film music**, and no composer, lyricist or playback singer beyond the four names attached to the **1931** recorded song and the **1935** playback innovation. No filmography, biography, later career or further song is given for any of them, and no claim is made about which of the two 1935 versions was completed first.
4. **No Indian animation history**, and no named Indian animation studio, film or practitioner.
5. **No figure of any kind** for Indian film output, screen counts, industry revenue, employment, box office, platform market share or a ranking against any other country.
6. **No year, cap, policy name or investment figure** for the foreign-investment provision in the film sector.
7. **No intake, admission process, fee, campus history, alumnus, head, director, protest or controversy** for either film-training institute.
8. **No holdings figure, reel count, title list, acquisition record, restoration project name, budget, staffing figure or facility description** for the film archive.
9. **No launch year, outlay, target, film count or project name** for the national film heritage mission.
10. **No completion date for the integration of the four merged film media units**, and **no asserted effect of the merger** on any function.
11. **No year in which Goa became the host of India's international film festival**, and **no award name or category of that festival**.
12. **No year of institution for the National Film Awards**, and **no complete list of its categories**.
13. **No recipient of the Dadasaheb Phalke Award or of any National Film Award category other than the three recorded with their dates** - the first recipient in 1969, and the award-year 2023 and award-year 2024 recipients.
14. **No category-by-category list of winners** for any National Film Awards edition.
15. **No Indian Academy Award recipient, nomination or entry is named**, and no claim is made about Indian cinema's record at the Academy Awards, at Cannes, at Venice or at Berlin beyond the characterisation of those bodies.
16. **No language, setting, producer, running time or festival history for the film named in the 2026 objective item**, beyond the award, its exact category and the direction credit.
17. **No section number for the piracy offences** in the current amending legislation, and **no notification, rule-making or commencement date** for it.
18. **No rule number, platform name, service name, enforcement instance, advisory, blocking order or litigation outcome** under the framework for online curated content, and **no outcome** of the challenges to its validity.
19. **No constitutional doctrine, free-speech test, reasonable-restriction analysis or case law** on film censorship; routed to the polity owner.
20. **No musical system content** - raga, tala, gharana or folk form - even where film song touches it; routed to the music owner.
21. **No stage form, traditional theatre repertoire or dramaturgy**; routed to the theatre owner.
22. **No monument conservation, UNESCO instrument or heritage-governance content**; routed to the heritage owner.
23. **No economic analysis** of the film industry, its market structure, its financing instruments or platform concentration; routed to the economy owner.

## Source-use statement

The core owner supplied this topic's ownership boundaries and syllabus fit, the composite-art frame, the essential film vocabulary, the institutional architecture by function, the award-and-festival distinction set, the complete history of Indian cinema from the 1896 exhibition to the 1939 regional entry, the certification and regulatory scheme with its current verified position, the social and regional reading, the preservation and soft-power cautions, and the entire answer architecture including the directive map, the qualified theses, the mark-scaled structures, the verdict scaffolds and the factual-risk controls. The enrichment owner supplied the four-fold characterisation of cinema as art form, industry, archive of social memory and mass medium, the six analytical dimensions, the case-study reading of regional and Northeast visibility, the five Mains-ready tensions and the advanced trap on international awards. The core OCR art and culture source supplied the chapter-level history in its own words, the complete talkies table with its qualifiers, the silent-era accompaniment, the companies, the entertainment tax, the 1926 landmark, the 1927 Committee, the parallel-cinema section, the women-in-cinema periodisation, the 1952 Act and CBFC sections in full, the cable-television boundary, the festivals directorate entry, the complete Dadasaheb Phalke Award entry, and the early-cinema and people's-theatre records in its theatre chapter.
